Sources: ZIP 316, ``Encoding of Unified Addresses'', ``Requirements for both
Unified Addresses and Unified Viewing Keys'' and ``Receivers''.
\end{definition}

The ascending order makes the raw encoding a function of the set of items,
so a representation that discards the order reproduces the same encoding,
provided it retains unrecognised items (ZIP 316, ``Rationale for Item
ordering''). This order is not the priority order below.

% compute/ua.py:
% [sec:addr-ua] item lengths: P2PKH 20 / 65 (chain code 32 + compressed key 33)
%   / 65; Sapling 43 / 128 (ak, nk, ovk, dk) / 64 (dk, ivk); Orchard 43 / 96
%   (ak, nk, rivk) / 64 (dk, ivk)
\begin{table}[tp]
\centering\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}llrrr@{}}
\toprule
Typecode & Item & Receiver & UFVK item & UIVK item \\
\midrule
$\mathtt{0x00}$ & transparent P2PKH & $20$ & $65$ & $65$ \\
$\mathtt{0x01}$ & transparent P2SH & $20$ & --- & --- \\
$\mathtt{0x02}$ & Sapling & $43$ & $128$ & $64$ \\
$\mathtt{0x03}$ & Orchard & $43$ & $96$ & $64$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Typecodes and item lengths of Revision~$0$, in bytes. In
Revision~$0$ Producers MUST NOT include typecode $\mathtt{0x01}$ in a UFVK
or UIVK, and Consumers MUST treat it as unrecognised there. The Priority
List of preference is $\mathtt{0x03}$, then $\mathtt{0x02}$, then
$\mathtt{0x01}$ or $\mathtt{0x00}$ (at most one of the two is present), the
reverse of the encoding order. Receiver lengths are those of
Definition~\ref{def:addr-raw}; the viewing-key lengths are those of
\S\ref{sec:addr-uvk}: $65 = 32 + 33$ (chain code and compressed
key), $128$ for $(\aksym, \nksym, \ovk, \dk)$, $64$ for $(\dk, \ivk)$, and
$96$ for $(\aksym, \nksym, \rivk)$ (ZIP 316, ``Encoding of Unified
Addresses'' and ``Encoding of Unified Full/Incoming Viewing Keys'').}
\label{tab:addr-typecodes}
\end{table}

Receiver selection. The Sender of a payment to a UA MUST use the receiver of
the most preferred type that it supports, by the Priority List Orchard
($\mathtt{0x03}$), Sapling ($\mathtt{0x02}$), then P2SH ($\mathtt{0x01}$)
or P2PKH ($\mathtt{0x00}$); an unrecognised item is treated as absent, with
the exception of \S\ref{sec:addr-rev2}. A wallet MAY let its users configure
which receiver types it can send to, and MUST NOT let them change the order
of the Priority List except by opting into an experiment, whose
specification MAY add to the list and SHOULD keep the preference for more
recent shielded protocols (ZIP 316, ``Encoding of Unified Addresses'',
``Transfers'' and ``Experimental Usage''). The Orchard receiver belongs to
the Orchard protocol, not to a pool: one $\ivk$ trial-decrypts both
Orchard-pool and Ironwood-pool note ciphertexts (ZIP 326, ``Scanning for
Ironwood-pool and Orchard-pool notes''), and the Ironwood pool changes no
address structure or encoding (ZIP 229, ``Abstract''). The pool in which a
payment to an Orchard receiver is made is fixed by the routing rule of
\S\ref{sec:pipeline-routing}, which carries the prohibition of ZIP 326,
``Wallet key-generation restrictions'', on paying external receivers in the
Orchard pool; the Priority List is unchanged.

\subsection{String encoding: F4Jumble and Bech32m}\label{sec:addr-string}

The Bech32m checksum does not cascade: a user who checks only the beginning
and the end of a long string can miss an adversarial substitution of an
inner receiver. ZIP 316 therefore passes the whole payload through F4Jumble,
an unkeyed four-round Feistel construction that approximates a random
permutation of the entire byte string, so that every output character
depends on every input byte. It states two goals (ZIP 316, ``Jumbling'').
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item \emph{Near second-preimage resistance.} Given $q$ honestly generated
  UAs or UVKs, an adversary does not produce a valid one that it controls,
  knowing at least one constituent private key, whose string matches one
  of the targets on a chosen set of characters, for concreteness the first
  $n$ and the last $m$.
\item \emph{Nonmalleability.} Without any private key, an adversary does
  not produce a valid UA or UVK usefully different from a target, for
  example with its shielded receiver removed, that matches the target on
  the checked characters.
\end{enumerate}
Generic attacks bound what is achievable: about $w\,c^{n+m}/q$ for~(i) and
$w/p$ for~(ii), where $w$ is the work to produce and verify one UA or UVK,
$c$ the size of the character set and $p$ the probability that a random
decoding is valid and useful (ZIP 316, ``Discussion''). Jumbling is not
authentication. F4Jumble is unkeyed, so anyone can jumble and checksum a
wholly substituted address, and an address is still obtained or verified
through a trusted channel; jumbling raises only the cost of partial-match
and malleation attacks against a genuine string.

% compute/ua.py:
% [sec:addr-string] F4Jumble: l_H = 64, l_MAX = (2^16 + 1) x 64 = 4194368
\begin{construction}[F4Jumble]\label{def:addr-f4jumble}
Let $\ell_H := 64$ and $\ell_{\mathsf{MAX}} := (2^{16} + 1)\,\ell_H =
4194368$. For $i \in \{0, 1\}$ and byte strings $u$, with the first argument
of BLAKE2b the personalisation,
\begin{align*}
  H_i(u) &:= \mathrm{BLAKE2b\text{-}}(8\ell_L)\bigl(
    \texttt{UA\_F4Jumble\_H} \,\|\, [i, 0, 0],\ u\bigr), \\
  G_i(u) &:= \text{the first } \ell_R \text{ bytes of }
    \mathop{\big\|}_{j=0}^{\lceil \ell_R / \ell_H \rceil - 1}
    \mathrm{BLAKE2b\text{-}512}\bigl(\texttt{UA\_F4Jumble\_G} \,\|\, [i]
    \,\|\, \mathsf{I2LEOSP}_{16}(j),\ u\bigr),
\end{align*}
an $\ell_L$-byte hash and an extendable-output function in the sense of the
\emph{Crypto Guide}, \S``Hash functions and the random oracle model'',
Definition ``Extendable-output function'', for the lengths $\ell_L$ and
$\ell_R$ below. For a byte string $M$ of length $\ell_M$ with
$38 \le \ell_M \le \ell_{\mathsf{MAX}}$, let
\[
  \ell_L := \min(\ell_H, \lfloor \ell_M / 2 \rfloor), \qquad
  \ell_R := \ell_M - \ell_L,
\]
split $M = a \,\|\, b$ with $\mathrm{length}(a) = \ell_L$, and let
\begin{align*}
  x &:= b \oplus G_0(a), & y &:= a \oplus H_0(x), \\
  d &:= x \oplus G_1(y), & c &:= y \oplus H_1(d),
\end{align*}
and $\mathsf{F4Jumble}(M) := c \,\|\, d$ (ZIP 316, ``Solution'').
Figure~\ref{fig:f4jumble} shows the four rounds.
\end{construction}

Each personalisation has $16$ bytes: $13 + 3$ for $H_i$ and $13 + 1 + 2$ for
$G_i$. They are pairwise distinct, differing at byte~$12$ between the $H$
and the $G$ family and at bytes~$13$ to~$15$ within a family, and distinct
from the three personalisations of key derivation in
Table~\ref{tab:zip32-leadbytes} and from that of $\mathsf{PRFexpand}$,
\texttt{Zcash\_ExpandSeed}. Being of one length and distinct, they are
prefix-free tags, so BLAKE2b under these personalisations is modelled as
independent random oracles (\emph{Crypto Guide}, \S``Domain separation and
personalisation'', Proposition ``Domain separation yields independent
oracles'').

\begin{figure}[tp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[y=0.8cm,
  font=\footnotesize, >={Stealth[length=1.8mm]},
  val/.style={draw=black!60, line width=0.6pt, rounded corners=2pt,
    minimum height=6mm, minimum width=14mm, align=center},
  fn/.style={draw=#1, fill=#1, fill opacity=0.14, text opacity=1,
    line width=0.7pt, rounded corners=2pt, minimum height=6mm,
    minimum width=11mm},
  xr/.style={circle, draw, line width=0.6pt, inner sep=0pt,
    minimum size=4mm},
  ln/.style={->, line width=0.6pt},
  lb/.style={font=\scriptsize, inner sep=1.5pt, align=center},
]
\def\L{0} \def\R{6}
\node[val] (a) at (\L,0) {$a$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $\ell_L$ bytes}};
\node[val] (b) at (\R,0) {$b$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $\ell_R$ bytes}};
% round 1: G_0 from a into b
\node[fn=tierin] (G0) at (3,-1.2) {$G_0$};
\node[xr] (x1) at (\R,-1.2) {$\oplus$};
\node[val] (x) at (\R,-2.3) {$x$};
% round 2: H_0 from x into a
\node[fn=tierfull] (H0) at (3,-3.4) {$H_0$};
\node[xr] (x2) at (\L,-3.4) {$\oplus$};
\node[val] (y) at (\L,-4.5) {$y$};
% round 3: G_1 from y into x
\node[fn=tierin] (G1) at (3,-5.6) {$G_1$};
\node[xr] (x3) at (\R,-5.6) {$\oplus$};
% round 4: H_1 from d into y
\node[fn=tierfull] (H1) at (3,-7.0) {$H_1$};
\node[xr] (x4) at (\L,-7.0) {$\oplus$};
\node[val] (c) at (\L,-8.3) {$c$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $\ell_L$ bytes}};
\node[val] (d) at (\R,-8.3) {$d$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $\ell_R$ bytes}};
% lanes
\draw[ln] (b) -- (x1);
\draw[ln] (x1) -- (x);
\draw[ln] (x) -- (x3);
\draw[ln] (x3) -- (d);
\draw[ln] (a) -- (x2);
\draw[ln] (x2) -- (y);
\draw[ln] (y) -- (x4);
\draw[ln] (x4) -- (c);
% taps
\draw[ln] (\L,-1.2) -- (G0);
\draw[ln] (G0) -- (x1);
\fill (\L,-1.2) circle (1.2pt);
\draw[ln] (\R,-3.4) -- (H0);
\draw[ln] (H0) -- (x2);
\fill (\R,-3.4) circle (1.2pt);
\draw[ln] (\L,-5.6) -- (G1);
\draw[ln] (G1) -- (x3);
\fill (\L,-5.6) circle (1.2pt);
\draw[ln] (\R,-7.0) -- (H1);
\draw[ln] (H1) -- (x4);
\fill (\R,-7.0) circle (1.2pt);
% round labels
\foreach \yy/\t in {-1.2/round 1, -3.4/round 2, -5.6/round 3,
    -7.0/round 4} {
  \node[lb, text=black!60, anchor=west] at (\R+0.9,\yy) {\t};
}
\node[lb, anchor=north] at (3,-8.9)
  {$\mathsf{F4Jumble}(a \,\|\, b) = c \,\|\, d$};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The four rounds of F4Jumble (Construction~\ref{def:addr-f4jumble})
on the unequal halves $a$ ($\ell_L$ bytes) and $b$ ($\ell_R$ bytes). Each
round replaces one half by its XOR with a function of the other half, which
it leaves unchanged: $G_0$ of $a$ into $b$ gives $x$, $H_0$ of $x$ into $a$
gives $y$, $G_1$ of $y$ into $x$ gives $d$, and $H_1$ of $d$ into $y$ gives
$c$. Blue boxes are the extendable-output functions $G_i$, with $\ell_R$
output bytes; amber boxes are the hashes $H_i$, with $\ell_L$ output bytes.
The names are those of the cells of Example~\ref{ex:ua-trace}. The
structure is the content of Proposition~\ref{prop:addr-f4jumble}.}
\label{fig:f4jumble}
\end{figure}

\begin{proposition}[F4Jumble is a permutation]\label{prop:addr-f4jumble}
For every $\ell_M$ with $38 \le \ell_M \le \ell_{\mathsf{MAX}}$,
$\mathsf{F4Jumble}$ is well defined and is a bijection of the $\ell_M$-byte
strings. Its inverse maps $c \,\|\, d$, with $\mathrm{length}(c) = \ell_L$,
to $a \,\|\, b$ computed by
\[
  y := c \oplus H_1(d), \quad x := d \oplus G_1(y), \quad
  a := y \oplus H_0(x), \quad b := x \oplus G_0(a).
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Well defined. From $\ell_M \ge 38$, $\ell_L \ge 19$; from $\ell_L \le 64$,
the output length $8\ell_L$ of $H_i$ is admissible for BLAKE2b. If
$\ell_M \le 128$ then $\ell_R \le 64$ and one block of $G_i$ suffices.
Otherwise $\ell_L = 64$ and $\ell_R = \ell_M - 64 \le 2^{16} \cdot 64$, so
$\lceil \ell_R / \ell_H \rceil \le 2^{16}$ and every counter $j$ fits
$\mathsf{I2LEOSP}_{16}$; this is the reason for
$\ell_{\mathsf{MAX}} = (2^{16} + 1)\,\ell_H$.

Bijection. Each of the four steps replaces one half by its XOR with a
function of the other half, which it leaves unchanged, and $r = p \oplus q$
implies $p = r \oplus q$. Each step is therefore inverted by recomputing the
same function of the unchanged half, and the composition of the four
inverses in reverse order is the stated map, a two-sided inverse. This is
the argument of the \emph{Crypto Guide}, \S``Format-preserving encryption
and FF1'', Proposition ``Feistel is a bijection regardless of the round
functions''. Unequal halves change nothing, since $G_i$ maps into $\ell_R$
bytes and $H_i$ into $\ell_L$ bytes, the lengths of the halves they are
XORed into.
\end{proof}

ZIP 316, ``Heuristic analysis'', motivates the number of rounds.

% compute/ua.py:
% [sec:addr-string] minimum F4Jumble^-1 input: Revision 2: 22 + 16 = 38 bytes
%   (one P2PKH item and padding); Revision 0: 48 bytes (ZIP 316); smallest
%   Revision 0 string input 45 + 16 = 61 > 48; conformance floor: l_M of at
%   least 256 bytes
\begin{construction}[Unified string encoding]\label{def:addr-string}
Human-readable parts of Revision~$0$: \texttt{u} for a UA, \texttt{uview}
for a UFVK and \texttt{uivk} for a UIVK on Mainnet; on Testnet
\texttt{test} is appended (\texttt{utest}, \texttt{uviewtest},
\texttt{uivktest}). Let $\mathsf{raw}$ be the raw encoding of
Definition~\ref{def:addr-container}, $\mathit{hrp}$ the human-readable part
and $\mathsf{padding}$ the human-readable part in US-ASCII right-padded
with zero bytes to $16$ bytes.
\begin{enumerate}
\item Producer. It MUST ensure $\mathrm{length}(\mathsf{raw}) \le
  \ell_{\mathsf{MAX}} - 16 = 4194352$, and outputs
  \[
    \mathrm{Bech32m}(\mathit{hrp}, \mathsf{F4Jumble}(\mathsf{raw} \,\|\,
    \mathsf{padding})),
  \]
  the Bech32m encoding of BIP 350 with its length restriction ignored.
\item Consumer. It MUST decode as follows, rejecting the string at the
  first failure:
  \begin{enumerate}[label=(\arabic*)]
  \item decode with Bech32m, rejecting an incorrect checksum;
  \item reject a decoded byte string shorter than $38$ or longer than
    $\ell_{\mathsf{MAX}}$ bytes;
  \item apply $\mathsf{F4Jumble}^{-1}$
    (Proposition~\ref{prop:addr-f4jumble});
  \item reject a result that does not end in the $16$-byte padding of the
    string's human-readable part, and remove that padding;
  \item parse the rest as a raw encoding, rejecting the whole string if
    any rule of Definition~\ref{def:addr-container} fails.
  \end{enumerate}
\end{enumerate}
UFVKs and UIVKs use the same procedure under their own human-readable parts
(ZIP 316, ``Encoding of Unified Addresses'', ``Encoding of Unified
Full/Incoming Viewing Keys'' and ``Usage for Unified Addresses, UFVKs, and
UIVKs''; BIP 350).
\end{construction}

The padding supplies the redundancy that defeats the generic
nonmalleability attack, since a random decoding must end in the exact
$16$ bytes, and gives the human-readable part the same protection against
malleation as the payload, which may help against cross-network
substitution and against confusion of an address with a viewing key (ZIP 316,
``Usage for Unified Addresses, UFVKs, and UIVKs'' and ``Heuristic analysis'').

Length rules (ZIP 316, ``Rationale for length restrictions'' and
``Efficiency''). The $38$-byte minimum is Revision~$2$'s: one P2PKH item of
$22$ bytes and the padding. Revision~$0$ specified $48$ bytes. Since only
typecodes $\mathtt{0x02}$ and $\mathtt{0x03}$ are shielded, no set of items
valid in Revision~$0$ has an encoding of $22$ to $31$ bytes (the smallest
Revision~$0$ input is $45 + 16 = 61$ bytes), so the difference is
unobservable except in the error reported, and a Consumer supporting
Revision~$2$ MAY apply either minimum to Revision~$0$ strings. ZIP 316
argues from the same reading. A Consumer that requires of a Revision~$0$
UA only an item that is not transparent, as the \textsf{zcash\_address}
crate does, and that applies the $38$-byte minimum accepts a $38$-byte input
holding one $22$-byte item of an unrecognised typecode, and observes the
difference. A Consumer
that cannot support strings up to $\ell_{\mathsf{MAX}}$ MUST support those
with $\ell_M$ of at least $256$ bytes; a full implementation supports
$\ell_{\mathsf{MAX}}$.

% compute/ua.py:
% [sec:addr-string] display: 20 - |'uview'| - |'1'| = 14 characters = 70 bits
%   of jumbled data
Display rule, specified (ZIP 316, ``Requirements for both Unified Addresses
and Unified Viewing Keys''). If the string of a UA or UVK is shown in an
abridged form, at least its first $20$ characters MUST be shown. The
rationale is the ZIP's (``Rationale for showing at least the first 20
characters''). After the longest Mainnet human-readable part of
Revision~$0$, \texttt{uview}, and the separator \texttt{1}, $14$ characters
remain, $70$ bits of jumbled data at $5$ bits per character, below the
target of $2^{125}$ against classical attacks, and multi-target attacks are
possible; $20$ is an absolute minimum and a wallet may show more. Only a
prefix counts, because displays that show different substrings let a user
compare only the characters common to all of them, and because checksum
characters complicate the analysis of partial matching.

% compute/ua.py:
% [sec:addr-string] item sizes: shielded receiver 1 + 1 + 43 = 45 bytes; P2PKH
%   receiver 1 + 1 + 20 = 22 bytes
Item sizes. A Sapling or Orchard receiver item has $1 + 1 + 43 = 45$ bytes
(typecode, length, $11$-byte diversifier and $32$-byte transmission key), a
P2PKH receiver item $1 + 1 + 20 = 22$ bytes, and the padding $16$ bytes.
Hence a UA with Sapling and Orchard receivers has $\ell_M = 106$, and one
with P2PKH, Sapling and Orchard receivers $\ell_M = 128$ (ZIP 316,
``Efficiency'').

% compute/ua.py:
% [sec:addr-string] ex:ua-trace: seed 0x00..0x1f, Mainnet, Orchard account 9
%   (m/32'/133'/9'), index j = 1
% [sec:addr-string]   dk    = 82cc9d79742fe5ae9a142b9336a98677b154fe20401eb18998dbed915b0453ce
% [sec:addr-string]   I2LEOSP_88(1) = 0100000000000000000000
% [sec:addr-string]   d_1, pk_d*, d_0, d_2: the values printed in the example
% [sec:addr-indices] FF1-AES256.Decrypt(dk, '', d_1) = LE_88(1), index 1;
%   pk_d = [ivk] DiversifyHash(d_1) holds for the external ivk and fails for
%   the internal one (whose dk_internal also decrypts d_1, to index
%   184814530803446824004762235)
% [sec:addr-string]   raw item = compactSize(0x03) || compactSize(43) || d_1
%   || pk_d* = 032b || ..., 45 bytes
% [sec:addr-string]   padding = 75 ('u') then 15 zero bytes; M = 61 bytes,
%   l_L = 30, l_R = 31
% [sec:addr-string]   a, b, x, y, d, c: the six cells printed in the example
% [sec:addr-string]   61 bytes -> 98 five-bit symbols (last padded by 2 zero
%   bits) + 6 checksum; 1 + 1 + 98 + 6 = 106 characters; equals the
%   zcash-test-vectors string for account 9, index 1; F4Jumble^-1(c || d) = M
\begin{example}[A unified address, end to end]\label{ex:ua-trace}
Let the seed be the $32$ bytes $\mathtt{0x00}, \mathtt{0x01}, \dots,
\mathtt{0x1f}$, on Mainnet, with the Orchard account $9$ at
$m_{\mathsf{Orchard}}/32'/133'/9'$ (Definition~\ref{def:zip32-account}),
the external scope and the index $j = 1$. Every value below is computed by
script from the seed.

\emph{Receiver.} The diversifier key $\dk$ is the first half of the
$\mathtt{0x82}$ expansion of the account's full viewing key, and FF1
encryption of $\mathrm{LE}_{88}(1)$ gives $d_1$
(Definition~\ref{def:addr-index}):
\begin{center}\footnotesize\ttfamily
\begin{tabular}{@{}l@{\quad}l@{}}
$\dk$ & 82cc9d79742fe5ae9a142b9336a98677b154fe20401eb18998dbed915b0453ce \\
$\mathsf{LEBS2OSP}_{88}(\mathrm{LE}_{88}(1))$ & 0100000000000000000000 \\
$d_1$ & 3fadf8edb20a3301e8260a \\
$\pkd^{\star}$
  & a311f4cbd54d7d6a76baac88c244b0b121c6dc22a8bcce15898e267829fc1e01 \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Decryption $\mathsf{FF1\text{-}AES256.Decrypt}(\dk, \texttt{""}, d_1)$
returns $\mathrm{LE}_{88}(1)$, so $j = 1$, and the ownership test
$\pkd = [\ivk]\,\mathsf{DiversifyHash}^{\mathsf{Orchard}}(d_1)$ of
Lemma~\ref{lem:addr-ownership} holds for the external $\ivk$. It fails for
the internal $\ivk_{\mathsf{int}}$, although $\dk_{\mathsf{int}}$ also
decrypts $d_1$, to the index $184814530803446824004762235$: recovery alone
proves nothing, and the test on $\pkd$ decides. The neighbouring
diversifiers are $d_0 = \mathtt{e340636542ece1c81285ed}$ and
$d_2 = \mathtt{987fd74a2256c596a66f83}$; that consecutive indices give
unrelated strings illustrates the \emph{Ironwood Guide}'s Assumption
``FF1-AES256 as a pseudorandom permutation'' (\S``Diversified addresses'') and
is not evidence for it.

\emph{Container.} The UA has one item, so its raw encoding
(Definition~\ref{def:addr-container}) is the header
$\mathrm{compactSize}(\mathtt{0x03}) \,\|\, \mathrm{compactSize}(43) =
\mathtt{032b}$ followed by the $43$-byte receiver $d_1 \,\|\,
\pkd^{\star}$, $45$ bytes in all. The padding is $\mathtt{75}$
(\texttt{u}) followed by fifteen zero bytes, so $M$ has $\ell_M = 61$,
$\ell_L = 30$ and $\ell_R = 31$. The cells of
Construction~\ref{def:addr-f4jumble}:
\begin{center}\footnotesize\ttfamily
\begin{tabular}{@{}l@{\quad}l@{}}
$a = M[0..30)$ & 032b3fadf8edb20a3301e8260aa311f4cbd54d7d6a76baac88c244b0b121 \\
$b = M[30..61)$
  & c6dc22a8bcce15898e267829fc1e0175000000000000000000000000000000 \\
$x = b \oplus G_0(a)$
  & e6f1529b03a0ad0a4209da8e4365ca14a5988bf1d03084a1c326f929c574ca \\
$y = a \oplus H_0(x)$
  & 37b358eb784bb2b315fd35c595628cfa9aa8045ef271728dd0cbf84dc81a \\
$d = x \oplus G_1(y)$
  & ad26d7fb043e09cbf18e8f4d80d2b423ecb01719170463d644be4c76daab6b \\
$c = y \oplus H_1(d)$
  & 98979f9a7d2dfce7b1616a8d2b9975f87878171b6fcf33b303f770b4aa64 \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The header, the diversifier and the padding are legible in $a$ and $b$,
and in neither $c$ nor $d$.

\emph{String.} The $61$ bytes of $c \,\|\, d$ regroup into $98$ five-bit
symbols, the last padded with two zero bits. With the human-readable part
\texttt{u}, the separator \texttt{1} and the $6$-symbol checksum, the
string has $1 + 1 + 98 + 6 = 106$ characters:
\begin{center}\scriptsize\ttfamily
u1nztelxna9h7w0vtpd2xjhxt4lpu8s9cmdl8n8vcr7actf2ny45nd\allowbreak
07cy8cyuhuvw3axcp545y0ktq9cezuzx84jyhex8dk4tdvwhu4dl
\end{center}
Decoding reverses each stage of Construction~\ref{def:addr-string};
$\mathsf{F4Jumble}^{-1}(c \,\|\, d) = M$. The string equals the unified-address
test vector for account~$9$ and index~$1$ of the zcash-test-vectors suite,
which ZIP 32, ``Test Vectors'', names.
\end{example}

\subsection{Unified viewing keys}\label{sec:addr-uvk}

\begin{definition}[Unified full and incoming viewing keys]\label{def:addr-uvk}
A UFVK and a UIVK are containers of Definition~\ref{def:addr-container}
under the string encoding of Construction~\ref{def:addr-string} with their
own human-readable parts; a Consumer MUST use the decoding procedure of
Construction~\ref{def:addr-string}. Their items, at the receiver typecodes,
are the following.
\begin{enumerate}
\item Orchard, $\mathtt{0x03}$: the $96$-byte raw full viewing key,
  respectively the $64$-byte raw incoming viewing key, of
  Definition~\ref{def:addr-raw}.
\item Sapling, $\mathtt{0x02}$: in a UFVK the $128$ bytes
  \begin{align*}
    &\mathsf{EncodeExtFVKParts}(\aksym, \nksym, \ovk, \dk) \\
    &\quad := \mathsf{LEBS2OSP}_{256}(\mathsf{repr}_{\mathbb{J}}(\aksym))
    \,\|\, \mathsf{LEBS2OSP}_{256}(\mathsf{repr}_{\mathbb{J}}(\nksym))
    \,\|\, \ovk \,\|\, \dk
  \end{align*}
  of ZIP 32, ``Sapling helper functions'', which SHOULD be derived from the
  account-level extended full viewing key; in a UIVK the $64$ bytes
  $\dk \,\|\, \mathsf{I2LEOSP}_{256}(\ivk)$, valid only if
  $\ivk \in \{1, \dots, 2^{251} - 1\}$ (protocol specification,
  \S``Sapling Incoming Viewing Keys''; ZIP 316 notes the exclusion of~$0$).
  The Sapling UIVK item carries $\dk$ beyond the specification's Sapling
  incoming viewing key, which is $\ivk$ alone (protocol specification,
  \S``Sapling Incoming Viewing Keys''); $\dk$ maps an index to a diversifier
  and back, so a UIVK yields addresses at indices
  (Construction~\ref{def:addr-ua-at-index}) and recovers the index of a
  diversifier (Lemma~\ref{lem:addr-ownership}).
\item Transparent P2PKH, $\mathtt{0x00}$: the $65$ bytes
  $\mathsf{c} \,\|\, \mathsf{ser}_P(\mathsf{pk})$ of a $32$-byte BIP 32
  chain code and a $33$-byte compressed public key, the last $65$ bytes of
  the BIP 32 extended public key serialisation (BIP 32, ``Serialization
  format''). The UFVK item is the key at $m/44'/\mathit{coin\_type}'/a'$,
  the account level of BIP 44, and the UIVK item its external (non-change)
  child at $m/44'/\mathit{coin\_type}'/a'/0$.
\end{enumerate}
The current viewing-key types reuse the receiver typecodes; future types MAY
use others. The Priority List does not govern viewing keys: a Consumer
normally takes the union of the information of all items (ZIP 316,
``Encoding of Unified Full/Incoming Viewing Keys'').
\end{definition}

The transparent item omits the BIP 32 depth, parent fingerprint and child
number; the format is ZIP 316's. That the child number of the account-level
key would reveal the account index is this volume's inference, not a
statement of the ZIP. The Sapling UFVK item carries no chain code, so a UFVK
supports no non-hardened Sapling child derivation below the account.

\begin{construction}[Attenuation of a UFVK to a UIVK]\label{def:addr-attenuate}
Item by item, with the typecode preserved:
\begin{enumerate}
\item a Sapling full viewing key to its incoming viewing key (protocol
  specification, \S``Sapling Key Components''), with $\dk$ carried over;
\item an Orchard full viewing key $(\aksym, \nksym, \rivk)$ to the
  external-scope incoming viewing key $(\dk, \ivk)$ (\emph{Ironwood Guide},
  \S``Viewing keys'');
\item a transparent P2PKH account key to its child at non-hardened index $0$
  by BIP 32 public-to-public derivation (BIP 32, ``Public parent key $\to$
  public child key'').
\end{enumerate}
Items with unrecognised typecodes, and items of experiments the user has
not opted into, MUST be dropped (ZIP 316, ``Deriving a UIVK from a
UFVK''). The treatment of metadata items is stated with them, in
\S\ref{sec:addr-rev2}.
\end{construction}

\begin{assumption}[Independence of the per-protocol key trees]%
\label{ass:addr-trees}
For a wallet seed $S$ (Definition~\ref{def:zip32-seed}) and an account
index $a$, the Orchard account key of Definition~\ref{def:zip32-account},
the Sapling extended spending key at $m_{\mathsf{Sapling}}/32'/
\mathit{coin\_type}'/a'$ of ZIP 32 and the BIP 32 extended private key at
$m/44'/\mathit{coin\_type}'/a'$ are jointly computationally
indistinguishable from three independently generated keys of the respective
protocols: a uniform Orchard spending key with a uniform chain code, a
uniform Sapling master-derived key and a uniform BIP 32 key.
\end{assumption}

The assumption has a heuristic basis and no proof. It follows if
BLAKE2b-512 under \texttt{ZcashIP32Sapling} (the Sapling master key) and
HMAC-SHA512 under the key \texttt{Bitcoin seed} (the BIP 32 master key) are
modelled as random oracles independent of BLAKE2b-512 under
\texttt{ZcashIP32Orchard}, and the Sapling and BIP 32 hardened child
derivations as PRFs keyed by the parent chain code (HMAC as a PRF:
\emph{Crypto Guide}, \S``PRFs from hash functions: length extension and
keyed BLAKE2''; ZIP 32, ``Sapling master key generation'' and ``Sapling
child key derivation''; BIP 32, ``Master key generation'' and ``Private
parent key $\to$ private child key''). Lemma~\ref{lem:zip32-prf} proves the
Orchard component and nothing more.

\begin{proposition}[Capabilities of a unified viewing key]%
\label{prop:addr-uvk-cap}
Let the keys of one account be generated from a seed, with
$\mathsf{use\_qsk} = \mathsf{false}$ for the Orchard component, and let
Assumption~\ref{ass:addr-trees} hold.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item From a UFVK the following are computed: for Orchard, the incoming and
  outgoing viewing keys of both scopes and $\nksym$, hence receipt, spend
  and outgoing detection (Proposition~\ref{prop:zip32-fvk}); for Sapling,
  the same through ZIP 32's internal key derivation; for transparent keys,
  every external and internal address and the transparent outgoing viewing
  keys (Construction~\ref{def:zip32-tovk}). No secret that is infeasible to
  compute from its own component becomes feasible from the whole UFVK: in
  particular the Orchard $\ask$ (\emph{Ironwood Guide}, \S``Key capabilities and
  scope'', Proposition ``Capability separation'', part~(a)), the Sapling spend
  authorising key and a transparent private key.
\item A UIVK gives receipt detection for the external scope in each
  shielded protocol it carries, and no spend detection for its shielded
  items: $\nksym$ is infeasible to compute from an Orchard $(\dk, \ivk)$
  (\emph{Ironwood Guide}, \S``Key capabilities and scope'', Proposition
  ``Capability separation'', part~(b)), and internal-scope receipt is neither
  detected (Proposition~\ref{prop:zip32-scopes}) nor derivable
  (Proposition~\ref{prop:zip32-capsep}). A transparent UIVK item yields the
  external addresses $m/44'/\mathit{coin\_type}'/a'/0/j$ and not the internal
  ones. Spends of transparent funds are public on the transparent chain, so
  their visibility is a property of the chain, not of the key.
\end{enumerate}
Metadata items confer no viewing authority (\S\ref{sec:addr-rev2}), so the
statements hold for containers of either revision.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Componentwise. The computations of~(a) and~(b) are those of the cited
constructions applied to each item. For the separations: by
Assumption~\ref{ass:addr-trees} the components are indistinguishable from
independently generated keys, so a reduction given one component generates
the others itself. An algorithm that outputs a secret of one protocol from
the whole container thus yields an algorithm that outputs it from that
protocol's component alone, with the advantage of
Assumption~\ref{ass:addr-trees} added; the success of either is efficiently
decidable, by recomputing the public key or viewing key from the output.
The per-component statements are Propositions~\ref{prop:zip32-fvk},
\ref{prop:zip32-scopes} and~\ref{prop:zip32-capsep} with the \emph{Ironwood
Guide}, \S``Key capabilities and scope'', Proposition ``Capability
separation'', under the assumptions named there, for Orchard; ZIP 32 and the
protocol specification, \S``Sapling Key Components'', cited, for Sapling;
and Assumption~\ref{ass:zip32-bip32} with BIP 32, ``Security'', for the
transparent keys. The transparent spend clause is a property of transparent
inputs, which name the outpoints they spend in the clear.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Deriving unified addresses}\label{sec:addr-derive}

\begin{construction}[Unified address at an index]\label{def:addr-ua-at-index}
Let $U$ be a UIVK. Choose one index $j \in \{0, \dots, 2^{88} - 1\}$, which
MUST be valid for every item of $U$:
\begin{enumerate}
\item for an Orchard item, always;
\item for a Sapling item, if and only if
  $\mathsf{DiversifyHash}^{\mathsf{Sapling}}(d_j) \neq \bot$, with $d_j$ the
  diversifier of the item's $\dk$ at $j$ (Definition~\ref{def:addr-index});
\item for a transparent P2PKH item, if and only if
  $0 \le j \le 2^{31} - 1$ and BIP 32 derivation of the child at index $j$
  succeeds; it fails with probability below $2^{-127}$ (BIP 32, ``Public
  parent key $\to$ public child key'').
\end{enumerate}
Each receiver is derived from its item at the same $j$: a Sapling or Orchard
receiver as the address at index $j$ of $(\dk, \ivk)$ (protocol
specification, \S``Sapling Key Components'' and \S``Orchard Key
Components''; \emph{Ironwood Guide}, \S``Diversified addresses'',
Construction ``Diversified address''), and a transparent P2PKH receiver as
the $\mathsf{hash160}$ of the non-hardened BIP 32 public child $j$ of the
external key, so that its path is $m/44'/\mathit{coin\_type}'/a'/0/j$
(BIP 44). Items with unrecognised typecodes, and items of experiments the
user has not opted into, MUST be dropped; metadata items are treated in
\S\ref{sec:addr-rev2} (ZIP 316, ``Deriving a Unified Address from a
UIVK'').
\end{construction}

% compute/ua.py:
% [sec:addr-derive] transparent item: valid indices j < 2^31, 2^31 of 2^88 =
%   2^-57 of the index space; at most 2^31 unified addresses
% [sec:addr-derive] Sapling item (seed 0x00..0x1f, account 9, indices
%   0..9999): 4527 valid, fraction 0.4527; predicted 8 (r_J - 1)/2^256 =
%   0.4528 (4 sigma = 0.020), not 1/2; indices 0..999: 454 valid
% [sec:addr-derive] Pr[a given index is Sapling-invalid] = 1 - 8 (r_J -
%   1)/2^256 = 0.5472; index 0 of this account is valid
\begin{proposition}[Indices valid for a unified key]%
\label{prop:addr-valid-indices}
Let $V(U)$ be the set of indices valid for every item of a UIVK $U$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item If $U$ has only an Orchard item, $V(U) = \{0, \dots, 2^{88} - 1\}$.
\item If $U$ has a transparent item, $V(U) \subseteq
  \{0, \dots, 2^{31} - 1\}$, a fraction $2^{31}/2^{88} = 2^{-57}$ of the
  index space, so $U$ has at most $2^{31}$ unified addresses.
\item Let $U$ have a Sapling item, and let $\mathrm{BLAKE2s\text{-}256}$ in
  $\mathsf{GroupHash}^{\mathbb{J}(r)*}$ be modelled as a random oracle, the
  model of the protocol specification, \S``Group Hash into Jubjub'', notes.
  The events that the indices are valid for the Sapling item are
  independent over distinct indices, each of probability
  \[
    \pi := \frac{h_{\mathbb{J}}\,(r_{\mathbb{J}} - 1)}{2^{256}}
      = \frac{8\,(r_{\mathbb{J}} - 1)}{2^{256}} \approx 0.4528,
  \]
  with $h_{\mathbb{J}} = 8$ the cofactor and $r_{\mathbb{J}}$ the prime
  subgroup order of Jubjub (protocol specification, \S``Jubjub''); an index
  is invalid with probability $1 - \pi \approx 0.5472$. ZIP 32's
  ``approximately half'' and ZIP 316's probability $1/2$ are this value
  rounded.
\item With a Sapling and a transparent item, $V(U)$ is the set of indices
  below $2^{31}$ that are Sapling-valid and at which BIP 32 derivation
  succeeds; its expected size lies within $2^{31} \cdot 2^{-127} = 2^{-96}$
  of $\pi \cdot 2^{31}$.
\end{enumerate}
For the Sapling key of account $9$ of the seed of
Example~\ref{ex:ua-trace}, $4527$ of the indices $0, \dots, 9999$ are
valid, a fraction $0.4527$, and index $0$ is valid.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The set $V(U)$ is the intersection of the per-item valid sets of
Construction~\ref{def:addr-ua-at-index}, which gives~(a) and~(b), distinct
indices giving distinct receivers.

(c)~Distinct indices give distinct diversifiers
(Proposition~\ref{prop:addr-distinct}~(a)), hence distinct inputs to
$\mathrm{BLAKE2s\text{-}256}$, whose outputs $h$ are then independent and
uniform on $\{0,1\}^{256}$. The group hash returns a point if and only if
$P := \mathsf{abst}_{\mathbb{J}}(h) \neq \bot$ and
$[h_{\mathbb{J}}]\,P \neq \mathcal{O}$ (protocol specification, \S``Group
Hash into Jubjub''). The map $\mathsf{abst}_{\mathbb{J}}$ reads $h$ as a
$255$-bit integer $v < q_{\mathbb{J}}$ and a sign bit $\tilde{u}$, rejecting
$v \ge q_{\mathbb{J}}$ and $v$ with no curve point (protocol specification,
\S``Jubjub''). Each point with $u \neq 0$ is the image of exactly one $h$,
since $u$ and $q_{\mathbb{J}} - u$ have different parities, and each of the
two points $(0, \pm 1)$ is the image of two. The curve has
$h_{\mathbb{J}} r_{\mathbb{J}}$ points and its $h_{\mathbb{J}}$-torsion
subgroup, the kernel of $[h_{\mathbb{J}}]$, has $8$ points: $(0, 1)$,
$(0, -1)$ and six with $u \neq 0$. The strings $h$ for which the hash
succeeds are thus in bijection with the points with $u \neq 0$ outside that
subgroup, of which there are $h_{\mathbb{J}} r_{\mathbb{J}} - 2 - 6 =
8\,(r_{\mathbb{J}} - 1)$, so $\pi = 8\,(r_{\mathbb{J}} - 1)/2^{256}$.

(d)~Parts~(b) and~(c) and Construction~\ref{def:addr-ua-at-index}~(3)
combined by linearity of expectation over the $2^{31}$ indices below
$2^{31}$, each of which fails BIP 32 derivation with probability below
$2^{-127}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Default addresses]\label{rem:addr-default}
ZIP 316 fixes neither an order in which a wallet searches for a valid index
nor a default unified address. The only normative defaults are ZIP 32's
per-protocol default diversifiers: for Sapling $d_j$ with $j$ the least
valid index, for Orchard $d_0$ (ZIP 32, ``Sapling diversifier derivation''
and ``Orchard diversifier and OVK derivation''). Issuing the unified address
at the least index valid for every item is designed but unspecified
(Definition~\ref{def:intro-classes}).
\end{remark}

Index~$0$ and recovery across wallets, specified (ZIP 316, ``Deriving a
Unified Address from a UIVK''). Index~$0$ is invalid for a Sapling item with
probability $1 - \pi$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:addr-valid-indices}), so a
wallet that requires a Sapling receiver often issues its first unified
address at a later index, while some wallets, earlier than ZIP~316 or
without a Sapling component in their unified addresses, always generate a
transparent P2PKH address at
index~$0$. Therefore every Zcash wallet, whether or not it supports unified
addresses, MUST assume that transparent funds may exist at diversifier
index~$0$ of each ZIP 32 account, even where it would never generate a
unified address at that index; reliable recovery of funds when key material
moves between wallets depends on this. Its use in restoration is stated in
\S\ref{sec:scan-keys}.

\subsection{Revision 2 containers}\label{sec:addr-rev2}

\begin{definition}[Metadata items]\label{def:addr-metadata}
The following is Revision~$2$ of ZIP 316, specified
(Definition~\ref{def:intro-classes}).
\begin{enumerate}
\item Human-readable parts. A UA is either \emph{shielded-only}, with
  human-readable part \texttt{zu}, which MUST NOT hold a transparent item
  (typecode $\mathtt{0x00}$ or $\mathtt{0x01}$), a Consumer rejecting any
  that does, or \emph{transparent-enabled}, with \texttt{tu}. A UFVK uses
  \texttt{uvf} and a UIVK \texttt{uvi}; on Testnet \texttt{test} is
  appended. The human-readable part distinguishes a Revision~$0$ container
  (\texttt{u}, \texttt{uview}, \texttt{uivk}) from a Revision~$2$ one.
\item Metadata items, typecodes $\mathtt{0xC0}$ to $\mathtt{0xFC}$
  (Definition~\ref{def:addr-container}), MAY affect the interpretation of
  the container, MUST NOT be selected as receivers and MUST NOT confer
  viewing authority.
\item MUST-understand items. Typecodes $\mathtt{0xE0}$ to $\mathtt{0xFC}$
  are MUST-understand: a Consumer that does not recognise such an item MUST
  regard the whole container as unsupported and not process it further.
  This is the exception to the ignore rule of
  Definition~\ref{def:addr-container}.
\item Registry. Typecodes $\mathtt{0x04}$ to $\mathtt{0xBF}$ are
  unassigned; $\mathtt{0xC0}$ to $\mathtt{0xDF}$ are metadata that need not
  be understood, none assigned; $\mathtt{0xE0}$ is the Address Expiry
  Height and $\mathtt{0xE1}$ the Address Expiry Time, permitted in
  \texttt{zu} and \texttt{tu} addresses and not in Revision~$0$;
  $\mathtt{0xE2}$ to $\mathtt{0xFC}$ are unassigned MUST-understand
  metadata; $\mathtt{0xFD}$ to $\mathtt{0xFFF9}$ are unassigned; and
  $\mathtt{0xFFFA}$ to $\mathtt{0xFFFF}$ are experimental. Producers MUST
  NOT include items whose typecodes are unassigned or not permitted for the
  type of the container.
\item Address expiry items. An item of typecode $\mathtt{0xE0}$ holds the
  \emph{Address Expiry Height}, an unsigned $32$-bit little-endian integer;
  a UA carrying it MUST be considered expired when the height of the chain
  exceeds that value. An item of typecode $\mathtt{0xE1}$ holds the
  \emph{Address Expiry Time}, an unsigned $64$-bit little-endian Unix time;
  a UA carrying it MUST be considered expired when the current time is
  after that value. A Producer MAY include either, both or neither.
\item Retroactive rule. A Revision~$0$ container MUST NOT hold a
  MUST-understand metadata item, and a Consumer MUST reject one that does.
\item Derivation. To derive a UIVK from a UFVK, or a UA from a UFVK or UIVK,
  a Consumer MUST understand every MUST-understand item of the source. The
  derived container MUST be Revision~$2$ if the source holds any metadata
  item, and SHOULD be Revision~$2$ if the source is. A UIVK derived from a
  UFVK MUST retain its $\mathtt{0xE0}$ and $\mathtt{0xE1}$ items unmodified.
  A UA derived from a UFVK or UIVK that carries $\mathtt{0xE0}$
  (respectively $\mathtt{0xE1}$) MUST carry $\mathtt{0xE0}$ (respectively
  $\mathtt{0xE1}$) with a value at most that of the viewing key. Metadata
  items that need not be understood and are unrecognised are dropped under
  Constructions~\ref{def:addr-attenuate} and~\ref{def:addr-ua-at-index}.
\item Containment. Revision~$2$ drops the rule that a UVK holds a shielded
  item, and admits a \texttt{tu} UA that consists of one P2PKH receiver
  (Definition~\ref{def:addr-container}). Revision~$2$ also admits a P2SH
  viewing-key item, which lies outside this volume;
  Table~\ref{tab:addr-typecodes} is that of Revision~$0$.
\end{enumerate}
Sources: ZIP 316, ``Revisions'', ``Metadata Items'', ``Address Expiration
Metadata'', ``Typecode Registry'', ``Requirements for both Unified Addresses
and Unified Viewing Keys'', ``Deriving a UIVK from a UFVK'' and ``Deriving a
Unified Address from a UIVK''.
\end{definition}

A Consumer that predates Revision~$2$ does not recognise the Revision~$2$
human-readable parts. Requiring MUST-understand items to appear only under
those parts therefore enforces the MUST-understand rule even against such a
Consumer (ZIP 316, ``Rationale for making Revision 0 UA/UVKs with
MUST-understand Typecodes invalid'').

Sender rules for expiring addresses, specified (ZIP 316, ``Address
Expiration Metadata'' and its ``Rationale''). The expiry height of a
transaction is its field $\mathsf{nExpiryHeight}$ (\emph{Ironwood Guide},
\S``The transaction format''). A Sender supporting Revision~$2$ MUST set a
nonzero $\mathsf{nExpiryHeight}$ in a transaction to a UA with an Address
Expiry Height, and MUST NOT send the transaction if the
$\mathsf{nExpiryHeight}$ it would otherwise construct exceeds that height;
lowering $\mathsf{nExpiryHeight}$ to the address's value instead would
reveal the destination's expiry on chain. With only an Address Expiry Time,
it SHOULD choose $\mathsf{nExpiryHeight}$ so that the transaction expires no
more than $24$ hours after the current time. With both items both
restrictions apply, and with several recipient UAs in one transaction all
their constraints apply. A wallet MUST NOT send to an address it knows to
have expired, and its error SHOULD suggest obtaining a current address. The
choice of $\mathsf{nExpiryHeight}$ is stated in \S\ref{sec:tx-expiry}.

Linkability through metadata (ZIP 316, ``Address Expiration Metadata'').
Diversified UAs derived from one UIVK that hold only Sapling or Orchard
receivers and no metadata are unlinkable (\S\ref{sec:addr-indices}); with
metadata items present this no longer holds, and it is RECOMMENDED that the
expiry height and time of distinct derived addresses vary. Re-issuing the
same receivers with different expiry metadata yields a visually distinct
address that is directly linkable to the original, since the receivers are
shared.

\subsection{Recipient addresses}\label{sec:addr-recipients}

\begin{definition}[Recipient address]\label{def:addr-recipient}
A \emph{recipient address} is one of the following:
\begin{enumerate}
\item a transparent address, P2PKH or P2SH, in its Base58Check encoding
  (Definition~\ref{def:addr-raw}~(f));
\item a Sapling payment address in its Bech32 encoding
  (Definition~\ref{def:addr-raw}~(e));
\item a unified address (Definition~\ref{def:addr-container},
  Construction~\ref{def:addr-string} and
  Definition~\ref{def:addr-metadata});
\item a \emph{TEX address} (transparent-source-only address): the Bech32m
  encoding, with human-readable part \texttt{tex} on Mainnet and
  \texttt{textest} on Testnet, of the $20$-byte validating-key hash of a
  P2PKH address. It is obtained from a Base58Check P2PKH address whose
  decoding has $22$ bytes beginning with the lead bytes
  $[\mathtt{0x1C}, \mathtt{0xB8}]$ (Testnet $[\mathtt{0x1D},
  \mathtt{0x25}]$) by removing them, and it is parsed by requiring the
  human-readable part and exactly $20$ decoded bytes (ZIP 320,
  ``Specification'').
\end{enumerate}
Sprout addresses are not recipient addresses: no transaction adds value to
the Sprout pool, and no typecode exists for them (\S\ref{sec:zip321-params};
ZIP 316, ``Requirements for both Unified Addresses and Unified Viewing
Keys''). A Sender paying a TEX address, as any output of a transaction, MUST
spend only transparent (P2SH or P2PKH) outputs in that transaction, and
SHOULD send only to transparent outputs (ZIP 320, ``Specification'';
\S\ref{sec:pipeline-tex}).

Two predicates on recipient addresses, defined here, are used by payment
construction and by ZIP 321 (ZIP 321, ``memo''; \S\ref{sec:zip321-params}).
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item An address is \emph{memo-capable} if and only if it is a Sapling
  payment address or a unified address that contains a Sapling or Orchard
  receiver, since a memo travels only in a note plaintext (\emph{Ironwood
  Guide}, \S``The note plaintext''). A memo reaches the recipient of a
  unified address only when the Sender selects a shielded receiver.
\item An address is \emph{transparent-only} if and only if it is a
  transparent or TEX address, or a unified address whose non-metadata items
  are all transparent receivers, which is possible only as a Revision~$2$
  \texttt{tu} address.
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\section{Compact blocks (ZIP 307)}\label{sec:sync-compact}

This section states the compact block as an abstract message format: the
records of a block and of a transaction, the compact outputs, spends and
Actions and the fields they omit, the compact variant of trial decryption
with the soundness of accepting a plaintext that no tag authenticates, and
the placement of notes in the note commitment trees from per-block tree
sizes. The service that delivers compact blocks is the subject of ``The
light-client service'' (\S\ref{sec:sync-service}).

\subsection{The compact block and the compact transaction}%
\label{sec:compact-format}

A \emph{compact block} carries only the data of a block that a wallet needs
to detect payments to its addresses, to detect spends of its notes and to
maintain the witnesses of its notes (ZIP 307, ``Compact Stream Format''); for
the transparent pool it carries, on request, the data needed to detect
spends of the wallet's transparent coins.

\begin{remark}[Normative status of the compact format]%
\label{rem:compact-status}
ZIP 307, ``Compact Stream Format'', ``Output Compression'' and ``Spend
Compression'', specifies the compact format for the Sapling pool only: a
compact block of height, block hash and transactions, and a compact
transaction of index, transaction identifier, spends and outputs. It names
neither the Orchard pool nor the Ironwood pool, nor per-block tree sizes,
nor transparent data. ZIP 318 and ZIP 326 address light wallets and specify
no compact format. The remainder of the format below (the parent hash of
a block, the tree sizes, the compact Actions of both
Orchard-protocol pools, the transparent part and pool selection) is designed
but unspecified (Definition~\ref{def:intro-classes}). For the compact
Actions, the rationale of ZIP 218, ``Rationale: Compact sync bandwidth per
action'', takes the compact form of the Actions of both pools to be one
message of the format served by light-client servers, with the fields and
the $148$-byte payload of Definition~\ref{def:compact-elements}; a
rationale is not normative, so the classification stands. The volume states
the format as an abstract message format, not as a serialisation, and
states no field number, field width or encoded size. The status of each ZIP
cited is that of Table~\ref{tab:intro-zips}.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}[Compact block]\label{def:compact-block}
A \emph{compact block} of height $h$ is a tuple
\[
  \mathit{CB} = (h,\, \mathsf{hash},\, \mathsf{prevHash},\,
    \mathsf{hdr},\, \mathsf{vtx},\, S)
\]
where
\begin{enumerate}
\item $h \in \N$ is the block height;
\item $\mathsf{hash}$ and $\mathsf{prevHash}$ are the $32$-byte hashes of the
  block and of its parent block (protocol specification, \S``Block Header
  Encoding and Consensus'');
\item $\mathsf{hdr}$ is either the encoded block header or empty; the
  abstract format does not guarantee that it is present;
\item $\mathsf{vtx} = (\mathit{ctx}_0, \dots, \mathit{ctx}_{m-1})$ is the
  sequence of compact transactions (Definition~\ref{def:compact-tx}) of the
  block, in block order;
\item $S = (S^{\mathsf{Sapling}}, S^{\mathsf{Orchard}},
  S^{\mathsf{Ironwood}})$ gives, for each shielded pool $\mathsf{pool}$, the
  number $S^{\mathsf{pool}}$ of leaves of that pool's note commitment tree
  after every transaction of the block has been appended, written
  $S^{\mathsf{pool}}_{\mathsf{end}}(h)$.
\end{enumerate}
Each $S^{\mathsf{pool}}$ is an integer in $[0, 2^{32}]$, where $2^{32}$ is
the leaf capacity of a tree of depth
$\mathsf{MerkleDepth}^{\mathsf{Sapling}} =
\mathsf{MerkleDepth}^{\mathsf{Orchard}} = 32$ (\emph{Ironwood Guide},
\S``The Merkle hash and the tree'', Definition ``Note commitment tree'';
protocol specification, \S``Constants''). The width of any serialisation of
$S^{\mathsf{pool}}$ lies outside the abstract format.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Compact transaction]\label{def:compact-tx}
A \emph{compact transaction} is a tuple
\[
  \mathit{ctx} = (i,\, \mathsf{txid},\, \mathit{SSp},\, \mathit{SOut},\,
    A^{\mathsf{Orchard}},\, A^{\mathsf{Ironwood}},\, T)
\]
where
\begin{enumerate}
\item $i \in \N$ is the index of the transaction in its block;
\item $\mathsf{txid}$ is the $32$-byte transaction identifier in protocol
  byte order, the form in which it is computed, not the byte-reversed form
  used for display (\emph{Ironwood Guide}, \S``Transaction digests and
  signatures'', Definition ``Transaction identifier''; protocol specification,
  \S``Transaction Identifiers'');
\item $\mathit{SSp}$ and $\mathit{SOut}$ are the sequences of compact Sapling
  spends and compact Sapling outputs, and $A^{\mathsf{Orchard}}$ and
  $A^{\mathsf{Ironwood}}$ the sequences of compact Actions of the Orchard
  pool and of the Ironwood pool (Definition~\ref{def:compact-elements}),
  each in the order of the transaction's descriptions, on which note
  positions depend (\S\ref{sec:compact-positions});
\item $T$ is an optional \emph{transparent part} $(O_{\mathsf{in}},
  O_{\mathsf{out}})$: $O_{\mathsf{in}}$ is the sequence of outpoints
  $(\mathsf{txid}', n)$ spent by the transaction's transparent inputs, in
  input order, and $O_{\mathsf{out}}$ the sequence of pairs
  $(\mathsf{value}, \mathsf{script})$ of its transparent outputs, in output
  order, with $\mathsf{value}$ in zatoshi.
\end{enumerate}
The coinbase transaction of a block is its first transaction and the one
whose single transparent input has a null previous outpoint (protocol
specification, \S``Coinbase Transactions''); it spends no outpoint, its
$O_{\mathsf{in}}$ is empty, and it is recognised by $i = 0$. No fee field is
part of the abstract format.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Pool selection]
Let $\mathcal{P} \subseteq \{\mathsf{transparent},\allowbreak
\mathsf{Sapling},\allowbreak \mathsf{Orchard},\allowbreak
\mathsf{Ironwood}\}$. The compact block
\emph{restricted to} $\mathcal{P}$ is obtained from a compact block by
keeping, in each compact transaction, only the components of the pools in
$\mathcal{P}$ ($\mathit{SSp}$ and $\mathit{SOut}$ for Sapling, the sequence
$A^{\mathsf{pool}}$ for an Orchard-protocol pool, and $T$ only when
$\mathsf{transparent} \in \mathcal{P}$), and by omitting every compact
transaction left with no component. The tuple $S$ is kept whole, and a
compact block is returned for every block of a requested range, possibly
with empty $\mathsf{vtx}$. The default is $\mathcal{P} = \{\mathsf{Sapling},
\mathsf{Orchard}, \mathsf{Ironwood}\}$. The range query of ``The query
interface'' (\S\ref{sec:service-queries}) takes $\mathcal{P}$ as an
argument. Pool selection is designed but unspecified
(Definition~\ref{def:intro-classes}); ZIP 307 includes by default only the
transactions that have Sapling spends or outputs (ZIP 307, ``Block header
validation'').
\end{definition}

For every shielded pool in $\mathcal{P}$ the restriction keeps every
commitment of that pool in its order, since an omitted compact transaction
has no component of the pool. The per-pool counts and offsets of
Definition~\ref{def:compact-position} are therefore the same on a
restricted block as on the unrestricted one.

\subsection{Compact outputs, spends, and Actions}%
\label{sec:compact-elements}

% compute/compact.py:
% [sec:compact-elements] compact Sapling spend = nf = 32 bytes
% [sec:compact-elements] compact Sapling output = cmu 32 + epk 32 + 52
%   = 116 bytes
% [sec:compact-elements] compact Action = nf 32 + cmx 32 + epk 32 + 52
%   = 148 bytes
\begin{definition}[Compact spend, output, and Action]%
\label{def:compact-elements}
Let $C_{\mathsf{enc}}$ be the note ciphertext of a Sapling output or of an
Action: $580$ bytes, the encryption of the $564$-byte note plaintext
followed by the $16$-byte tag. Its split at the offsets $52$ and $564$ is
that of the \emph{Ironwood Guide}, \S``Transaction digests and signatures'',
Definition ``Transaction identifier''.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item A \emph{compact Sapling spend} is the $32$-byte nullifier
  $\nfsym$ of a Spend description.
\item A \emph{compact Sapling output} is the triple
  $(\mathsf{cmu},\allowbreak \mathsf{ephemeralKey},\allowbreak
  C_{\mathsf{enc}}[0{:}52])$, a payload of $32 + 32 + 52 = 116$ bytes.
\item A \emph{compact Action}, of the Orchard pool and of the Ironwood pool
  alike, is the tuple $(\nfsym, \cmx, \mathsf{ephemeralKey},
  C_{\mathsf{enc}}[0{:}52])$, a payload of $32 + 32 + 32 + 52 = 148$ bytes.
\end{enumerate}
Parts~(a) and~(b) are specified (ZIP 307, ``Output Compression'' and
``Spend Compression''); part~(c) is designed but unspecified
(Definition~\ref{def:intro-classes}). The fields are those of the protocol
specification, \S``Spend Description Encoding and Consensus'', \S``Output
Description Encoding and Consensus'' and \S``Action Description Encoding
and Consensus''. Only payload sizes are stated; the size of any
serialisation lies outside the abstract format.
\end{definition}

The nullifier $\nfsym$ of a compact Action has two roles. It detects a spend
of the note that the Action consumes, and it is the element $\rho$ of the
note that the Action creates (\emph{Ironwood Guide}, \S``Nullifier
chaining'', Construction ``Chaining rule''), an input of $\mathsf{esk}$,
$\psi$ and $\rcm$ and hence of the commitment that acceptance recomputes
(Definition~\ref{def:compact-accept}). A compact Action without $\nfsym$
therefore cannot be accepted.

A compact block omits the memo $C_{\mathsf{enc}}[52{:}564]$, the tag
$C_{\mathsf{enc}}[564{:}580]$, the outgoing ciphertext $C_{\mathsf{out}}$,
value commitments, randomised validating keys, anchors, proofs and
signatures (ZIP 307, ``Output Compression'', ``Spend Compression'' and
``Transaction privacy''). Recovery of a memo or of an outgoing plaintext
requires the full transaction, fetched by its identifier (``Leakage of
transaction queries'', \S\ref{sec:priv-txq}).

\begin{proposition}[Sufficiency of the compact fields]%
\label{prop:compact-sufficient}
Let $\mathit{CB}$ be a compact block of height $h$, and let a wallet hold
incoming viewing keys and the nullifiers of its notes.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item Receipt detection, that is compact decryption and acceptance of an
  output (\S\ref{sec:compact-decrypt}), is a function of the key, of $h$,
  and of the record $(\mathsf{cmu}, \mathsf{ephemeralKey},
  C_{\mathsf{enc}}[0{:}52])$ of a Sapling output or
  $(\nfsym, \cmx, \mathsf{ephemeralKey}, C_{\mathsf{enc}}[0{:}52])$ of an
  Action.
\item Spend detection is a function of the fields $\nfsym$ of
  $\mathit{SSp}$, $A^{\mathsf{Orchard}}$ and $A^{\mathsf{Ironwood}}$.
\item Note placement is a function of the order of the commitments in
  $\mathsf{vtx}$ and of $S$ (\S\ref{sec:compact-positions}).
\end{enumerate}
No field omitted by the compact block enters~(i) to~(iii).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(i)~Trial decryption and acceptance read the ephemeral key, the ciphertext
prefix that encrypts the lead byte, diversifier, value and note seed, the
commitment, the height through the admissible lead bytes, and, for an
Action, $\nfsym$ as $\rho$ (\emph{Ironwood Guide}, \S``Trial decryption and
note acceptance'', Constructions ``Trial decryption'' and ``Acceptance of an
Ironwood-pool output''; ZIP 307, ``Scanning for relevant transactions'' for
Sapling); the memo and the tag enter only the full decryption.
(ii)~A spend of a note is detected by membership of its nullifier among the
published ones (ZIP 307, ``Detecting spends'').
(iii)~The chain appends each pool's commitments in block, transaction and
description order to the least unused leaf (\emph{Ironwood Guide},
\S``The Merkle hash and the tree'', Definition ``Note commitment tree'';
protocol specification, \S``Note Commitment Trees''), so positions follow from
that order and a tree size.
\end{proof}

The omitted fields serve the verification of the chain, which the client
delegates to the server; that delegation and its assumption are stated in
``Architecture and authority'' (\S\ref{sec:service-trust}).

\subsection{Compact trial decryption}\label{sec:compact-decrypt}

Trial decryption and acceptance of full outputs are those of the
\emph{Ironwood Guide}, \S``Trial decryption and note acceptance''
(Constructions ``Trial decryption'' and ``Acceptance of an Ironwood-pool
output''), cited and not re-derived. This subsection states the compact
variant.

% compute/compact.py:
% [sec:compact-decrypt] compact note plaintext = 1 (lead byte) + 11 (d)
%   + 8 (v) + 32 (rseed) = 52 bytes = C_enc[0..52); the memo follows to
%   564, the tag to 580
\begin{definition}[Compact note plaintext]\label{def:compact-np}
The \emph{compact note plaintext} is the tuple
$\mathsf{np}_{\mathsf{c}} = (\mathsf{leadByte}, d, v, \mathsf{rseed})$,
encoded as
\[
  \mathsf{leadByte} \,\|\, \mathsf{LEBS2OSP}_{88}(d) \,\|\,
  \mathsf{I2LEOSP}_{64}(v) \,\|\, \mathsf{rseed},
\]
of $1 + 11 + 8 + 32 = 52$ bytes: the note plaintext of the \emph{Ironwood
Guide}, \S``The note plaintext'', Definition ``Note plaintext'', without its
memo (protocol
specification, \S``Note Plaintexts and Memo Fields'' and \S``Encodings of
Note Plaintexts and Memo Fields''). The AEAD encryption is a stream-cipher
encryption followed by a tag, so $C_{\mathsf{enc}}[0{:}52]$ is the
encryption of exactly these $52$ bytes, which are the contents and the
opening of the note commitment (ZIP 307, ``Output Compression'').
\end{definition}

\begin{construction}[Compact decryption of an output]\label{def:compact-trial}
Input: an incoming viewing key $\ivk$ of the pool's protocol and a compact
output or compact Action with fields $\mathsf{ephemeralKey}$ and
$c = C_{\mathsf{enc}}[0{:}52]$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item Compute $\mathsf{epk} := \mathsf{abst}(\mathsf{ephemeralKey})$ on the
  pool's curve, $\mathsf{abst}_{\mathbb{J}}$ on Jubjub for Sapling and
  $\mathsf{abst}_{\PP}$ on Pallas for either Orchard-protocol pool; return
  $\bot$ if $\mathsf{epk} = \bot$.
\item Compute $K := \mathsf{KDF}(\mathsf{KA.Agree}(\ivk, \mathsf{epk}),
  \mathsf{ephemeralKey})$ with the key agreement and key derivation of the
  pool's protocol.
\item Compute $\mathsf{np}_{\mathsf{c}} := c \oplus
  \mathrm{KS}_1(K, 0^{96}, 52)$, where $\mathrm{KS}_1(K, 0^{96}, 52)$ is the
  first $52$ bytes of the ChaCha20 keystream under key $K$ and the
  all-zero nonce $0^{96}$ of the protocol specification,
  \href{https://zips.z.cash/protocol/protocol.pdf\#concretesym}{\S``Symmetric
  Encryption''}, starting at block counter~$1$, the counter from which
  ChaCha20-Poly1305 encrypts (block~$0$ yields the Poly1305 key;
  \emph{Crypto Guide}, \S``The ChaCha20-Poly1305 AEAD'', Construction
  ``ChaCha20-Poly1305''; RFC 8439, \S2.8); equivalently, bytes $64$ to
  $115$ of the keystream from counter~$0$.
\item Return $\mathsf{np}_{\mathsf{c}}$ parsed as a compact note plaintext
  (Definition~\ref{def:compact-np}).
\end{enumerate}
The tag is never checked. The result is a \emph{candidate}, not an accepted
note (ZIP 307, ``Scanning for relevant transactions'').
\end{construction}

% compute/compact.py:
% [sec:compact-decrypt] ZIP212GracePeriod = 32,256 blocks (specification
%   constant): lead bytes 0x01 and 0x02 both accepted for Sapling notes
%   during it
\begin{definition}[Acceptance of a compact candidate]\label{def:compact-accept}
Let $\mathsf{np}_{\mathsf{c}} = (\mathsf{leadByte}, d, v, \mathsf{rseed})$
be the candidate returned by Construction~\ref{def:compact-trial} under
$\ivk$ for an output of pool $\mathsf{pool}$ in a compact block of height
$h$. Let $H_{\mathsf{C}}$ be the Canopy activation height and
$\mathsf{ZIP212GracePeriod} = 32{,}256$ blocks (protocol specification,
\S``Constants''). The set of admissible lead bytes is
\[
  \mathsf{allowedLeadBytes}^{\mathsf{pool}}(h) :=
  \begin{cases}
    \{\mathtt{0x01}\} & \text{if } h < H_{\mathsf{C}}, \\
    \{\mathtt{0x01}, \mathtt{0x02}\} & \text{if } H_{\mathsf{C}} \le h <
      H_{\mathsf{C}} + \mathsf{ZIP212GracePeriod}, \\
    \{\mathtt{0x02}\} & \text{otherwise, if } \mathsf{pool} \neq
      \mathsf{Ironwood}, \\
    \{\mathtt{0x03}\} & \text{otherwise}
  \end{cases}
\]
(protocol specification, \S``Note Plaintexts and Memo Fields''), so that
an Orchard-pool note has lead byte $\mathtt{0x02}$ and an Ironwood-pool
note $\mathtt{0x03}$. The wallet accepts $\mathsf{np}_{\mathsf{c}}$ if and
only if every check passes:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item $\mathsf{leadByte} \in \mathsf{allowedLeadBytes}^{\mathsf{pool}}(h)$;
  for Sapling, moreover, $\rcm < r_{\mathbb{J}}$ and
  $\gd := \mathsf{DiversifyHash}(d) \neq \bot$, with $\rcm := \mathsf{rseed}$
  read as an integer for lead byte $\mathtt{0x01}$;
\item with $\pkd := [\ivk]\,\gd$, and with $\rho := \nfsym$ of the same
  Action for either Orchard-protocol pool, the note commitment recomputed
  from $(\gd, \pkd, v, \rho, \psi, \rcm)$, where $\psi$ and $\rcm$ are
  derived from $\mathsf{rseed}$ as the pool and the lead byte prescribe
  ($\rho$ and $\psi$ absent for Sapling), is not $\bot$ and extracts to the
  record's $\mathsf{cmu}$ (Sapling) or $\cmx$ (either Orchard-protocol
  pool);
\item for every lead byte other than $\mathtt{0x01}$, the field
  $\mathsf{ephemeralKey}$ equals the encoding of $[\mathsf{esk}]\,\gd$, with
  $\mathsf{esk}$ re-derived from $\mathsf{rseed}$, and from $\rho$ for
  either Orchard-protocol pool.
\end{enumerate}
The accepted note is $n = ((d, \pkd), v, \rho, \psi, \rcm)$ without memo,
with $\rho$ and $\psi$ absent for a Sapling note. The derivations of $\psi$,
$\rcm$ and $\mathsf{esk}$ are cited and not restated: for Sapling and
the Orchard pool to the protocol specification, \S``Decryption using an
Incoming Viewing Key (Sapling and Orchard)'' (ZIP 212, ``Changes to the
process of sending Sapling or Orchard notes''); for the Ironwood pool to the
\emph{Ironwood Guide}, \S``Trial decryption and note acceptance'', Construction
``Acceptance of an Ironwood-pool output'', whose checks~(i), (ii) with~(iv),
and~(iii) are checks~(i), (ii) and~(iii) here (ZIP 2005, ``Changes to the
Protocol Specification''). The method of comparison is not part of the rule.
\end{definition}

Check~(iii) is a step of the specification's decryption procedure and of
ZIP 307, not an addition (ZIP 212, ``Changes to the process of receiving
Sapling or Orchard notes''). For an Ironwood-pool note it is made directly
after the computation of $\gd$, in the order of ZIP 2005. Without it a
sender could agree the key with one address of a recipient while naming
the diversifier of another, and link the two addresses by observing
acceptance (\emph{Ironwood Guide}, \S``Trial decryption and note acceptance'',
Remark ``Role of check (iii)''; ZIP 212, ``Motivation''). A Sapling note with
lead byte $\mathtt{0x01}$, created under the rules before ZIP 212, skips
check~(iii), since its $\mathsf{esk}$ is not derivable from $\mathsf{rseed}$.

For a note $n$ the \emph{commitment input} is the pair of committed values
and trapdoor from which check~(ii) computes its commitment:
$((\gd, \pkd, v), \rcm)$ for Sapling and $((\gd, \pkd, v, \rho, \psi),
\rcm)$ for either Orchard-protocol pool. An \emph{address of} $\ivk$ is a
pair $(d, [\ivk]\,\mathsf{DiversifyHash}(d))$ with
$\mathsf{DiversifyHash}(d) \neq \bot$.

\begin{proposition}[Soundness of compact acceptance]\label{prop:compact-sound}
Let $\mathit{cm}^{*}$ be the commitment, $\mathsf{cmu}$ or $\cmx$, that a
compact output or compact Action carries.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item Every note $n$ accepted by Definition~\ref{def:compact-accept} is an
  opening of $\mathit{cm}^{*}$. For every efficient sender, which chooses
  $\mathsf{ephemeralKey}$, $C_{\mathsf{enc}}$ and the keys under which they
  are formed, the probability that the wallet accepts $n$ while the sender
  outputs a note $n'$ whose commitment input differs from that of $n$ and
  whose commitment extracts to $\mathit{cm}^{*}$ is negligible, under the
  binding of the pool's note commitment scheme. The tag of the AEAD gives no
  integrity against the sender, who holds $K$, so its omission loses none.
\item For an output honestly formed for a note $n'$ whose address is not an
  address of $\ivk$, the probability that $\ivk$ accepts a candidate is
  negligible under the same assumption.
\end{enumerate}
Whether $\mathit{cm}^{*}$ is the commitment of an output of the best chain
is not a property of decryption; it is settled under the assumption of
``Architecture and authority'' (\S\ref{sec:service-trust}).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(a)~Check~(ii) recomputes the commitment of $n$ deterministically from its
commitment input and compares it with $\mathit{cm}^{*}$, whatever the
ciphertext and whichever key decrypted it, so $n$ opens $\mathit{cm}^{*}$.
Consider the algorithm that generates the wallet's key, runs the sender and
the compact decryption and acceptance, and outputs the commitment inputs of
$n$ and $n'$. When the event of~(a) occurs it outputs two distinct
commitment inputs whose commitments extract to the same
$\mathit{cm}^{*} \neq \bot$. For either Orchard-protocol pool this is
excluded, except with negligible probability, by the argument of the
\emph{Ironwood Guide}, \S``Trial decryption and note acceptance'', Proposition
``Consistency of accepted notes'', part~(c), under Proposition ``Hiding and
binding of note commitments'' (\S``Note commitments''). That proof uses neither
the integrity of ChaCha20-Poly1305 nor key commitment, so it applies unchanged
to a candidate decrypted from the $52$-byte prefix; and binding holds for every
$\rcm$, hence also for the trapdoor derivation of the Orchard pool. For Sapling
the named assumption is the computational binding of
$\NoteCommit^{\mathsf{Sapling}}$, the security requirement of the protocol
specification, \S``Windowed Pedersen commitments'', together with the
injectivity of $\Extract_{\mathbb{J}^{(r)}}$ on $\mathbb{J}^{(r)}$ (protocol
specification, \S``Coordinate Extractor for Jubjub'').

(b)~Let $n$ be accepted. By check~(ii) its address is
$(d, [\ivk]\,\gd)$ with $\gd = \mathsf{DiversifyHash}(d) \neq \bot$, an
address of $\ivk$. Suppose the commitment inputs of $n$ and $n'$ were equal.
Then the diversified base $\mathsf{DiversifyHash}(d')$ of the address
$(d', \pkd')$ of $n'$ equals $\gd$, and $\pkd' = [\ivk]\,\gd =
[\ivk]\,\mathsf{DiversifyHash}(d')$, so $(d', \pkd')$ is an address of
$\ivk$, contrary to the hypothesis. The commitment inputs therefore differ,
and the honest sender outputs $n'$, whose commitment extracts to
$\mathit{cm}^{*}$; the honest sender is an efficient sender of~(a), which
bounds the probability.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Note positions and tree sizes}\label{sec:compact-positions}

For each shielded pool, every note commitment of a block, not only those of
the wallet's notes, is appended to the wallet's copy of that pool's note
commitment tree in block order, transaction order and description order:
$\cmx$ of each Action of the pool and $\mathsf{cmu}$ of each Sapling output
(ZIP 307, ``Creating and updating note witnesses''; \emph{Ironwood Guide},
\S``The Merkle hash and the tree'', Definition ``Note commitment tree'';
protocol specification, \S``Note Commitment Trees''). The leaf of each accepted
note is marked for witness retention. The part of the tree a wallet must retain
is fixed in ``Commitment-tree synchronisation'' (\S\ref{sec:sync-tree}).

\begin{definition}[Note position]\label{def:compact-position}
Let $\mathsf{pool}$ be a shielded pool, $b$ a block height with compact block
$\mathit{CB}$, and $n_{\mathsf{pool}}(b)$ the number of commitments of
$\mathsf{pool}$ in $\mathit{CB}$. The \emph{start size} of $b$ is
\[
  S^{\mathsf{pool}}_{\mathsf{start}}(b) :=
  \begin{cases}
    S^{\mathsf{pool}}_{\mathsf{end}}(b - 1) & \text{if the wallet knows
      it}, \\
    0 & \text{otherwise, if } b \text{ lies below the activation of }
      \mathsf{pool}, \\
    S^{\mathsf{pool}}_{\mathsf{end}}(b) - n_{\mathsf{pool}}(b) &
      \text{otherwise},
  \end{cases}
\]
the wallet knowing $S^{\mathsf{pool}}_{\mathsf{end}}(b - 1)$ when it holds
the compact block of height $b - 1$ or a tree state of $\mathsf{pool}$ at
the end of $b - 1$; in the third case the value is taken from $\mathit{CB}$
alone, and the block is rejected if it is negative. For the $j$-th output
$o$ of $\mathsf{pool}$, counted from $j = 0$, of a compact transaction
$\mathit{ctx}$ of $b$,
\[
  \mathsf{position}(o) := S^{\mathsf{pool}}_{\mathsf{start}}(b) +
    \mathsf{off}(\mathit{ctx}) + j,
\]
where $\mathsf{off}(\mathit{ctx})$ is the number of commitments of
$\mathsf{pool}$ in the compact transactions of $b$ before $\mathit{ctx}$.
Positions are per pool. A block scanned out of order thus places its notes
from its own compact block. Note positions are designed but unspecified
(Definition~\ref{def:intro-classes}).
\end{definition}

For a pool outside the pool set $\mathcal{P}$ of the query that returned
$b$, the count $n_{\mathsf{pool}}(b)$ is not served, and neither position
nor check is defined.

The position of a note is the leaf index of its commitment (\emph{Ironwood
Guide}, \S``The Merkle hash and the tree'', Definition ``Note commitment tree'',
item~1). Every spend needs it
for the authentication path. A Sapling nullifier depends on it, through
$\rho = \mathsf{MixingPedersenHash}(\cmsym, \mathsf{pos})$ (protocol
specification, \S``Computing $\rho$ values and Nullifiers''), while the
nullifier of an Orchard-protocol note does not.

\begin{lemma}[Consistency of tree sizes]\label{lem:compact-sizes}
Let the wallet reject a block $b$ unless, for each shielded pool of the
pool set $\mathcal{P}$ of the query that returned $b$,
\[
  S^{\mathsf{pool}}_{\mathsf{end}}(b) \ge n_{\mathsf{pool}}(b)
  \quad\text{and}\quad
  S^{\mathsf{pool}}_{\mathsf{start}}(b) + n_{\mathsf{pool}}(b) =
  S^{\mathsf{pool}}_{\mathsf{end}}(b).
\]
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item If every reported size is the true size, no block is rejected, and
  every position of Definition~\ref{def:compact-position} is the leaf index
  of the commitment in the pool's tree.
\item A reported $S^{\mathsf{pool}}_{\mathsf{end}}(b)$ that is inconsistent
  with the block's own commitment count, or with the known end size of
  $b - 1$, is rejected.
\item A block with $n_{\mathsf{pool}}(b) > 0$ and reported size $0$ is
  rejected. A size read as zero therefore yields a position only where $0$
  is consistent with the block, and the check needs no means of telling an
  absent size from zero.
\item A size that is false but consistent with both conditions is not
  detected; its exclusion is clause~(a) of the server assumption of
  ``Architecture and authority'' (\S\ref{sec:service-trust}).
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(a)~The chain appends the commitments of a pool in block, transaction and
description order, each to the least unused index (\emph{Ironwood Guide},
\S``The Merkle hash and the tree'', Definition ``Note commitment tree''). The
true start size of $b$ is thus the true end size of $b - 1$, which equals
$S^{\mathsf{pool}}_{\mathsf{end}}(b) - n_{\mathsf{pool}}(b) \ge 0$, so both
conditions hold in every case of the start size; and the $j$-th commitment of
$\mathit{ctx}$ lands at index $S^{\mathsf{pool}}_{\mathsf{start}}(b) +
\mathsf{off}(\mathit{ctx}) + j$. (b)~A size $S^{\mathsf{pool}}_{\mathsf{end}}(b)
< n_{\mathsf{pool}}(b)$ fails the first condition. With a known start size, a
size that differs from $S^{\mathsf{pool}}_{\mathsf{start}}(b) +
n_{\mathsf{pool}}(b)$ fails the second. (c)~A size $0$ with
$n_{\mathsf{pool}}(b) > 0$ fails the first condition. (d)~Let the wallet not
know the end size of $b - 1$, and let $b$ lie at or above the activation of
$\mathsf{pool}$, so that its start size is that of the third case of
Definition~\ref{def:compact-position}. Adding one integer $\delta \neq 0$ to
$S^{\mathsf{pool}}_{\mathsf{end}}(b)$ and to the end sizes of all later scanned
blocks, such that every shifted size stays in $[0, 2^{32}]$ and at least the
count of its block, preserves both conditions for $b$, whose start size is
computed from its own end size, and for every later block, whose start and end
sizes shift by $\delta$ together.
\end{proof}

Comparison of the running position with $S^{\mathsf{pool}}_{\mathsf{end}}(b)$
identifies the last commitment of $\mathsf{pool}$ in $b$, at position
$S^{\mathsf{pool}}_{\mathsf{end}}(b) - 1$. The tree after that commitment,
or for $n_{\mathsf{pool}}(b) = 0$ the tree at the end of $b - 1$, is the
tree state at the end of $b$. Its root is the anchor that a later spend
against height $b$ uses (\emph{Ironwood Guide}, \S``Anchors''; protocol
specification, \S``Transactions and Treestates''), and the wallet records
it as a candidate anchor.

\section{The light-client service}\label{sec:sync-service}

This section states the server from which a light wallet obtains chain data.
It fixes the architecture and the one assumption on the server that the
volume uses, states which checks a light client can make on the server's
responses and what the server can fabricate or omit undetected, and defines
the service as an abstract interface of typed queries on the chain, the note
commitment trees, transactions, submission and transparent addresses.

\subsection{Architecture and authority}\label{sec:service-trust}

\begin{definition}[Light-client architecture]\label{def:service-arch}
A \emph{light-client system} has three components:
\begin{enumerate}
\item a \emph{full node}, which validates the chain and is the root of trust
  for chain state;
\item a \emph{proxy}, which extracts from the node's view of the best chain
  the data of compact blocks (Definition~\ref{def:compact-block}) and
  answers queries;
\item a \emph{light client}, which holds keys and no copy of the chain,
  queries the proxy, and maintains its own view of chain state and of its
  notes.
\end{enumerate}
This volume calls the node and the proxy together the \emph{server}. The
wallet of ``Parties, pools, and classifications'' (\S\ref{sec:intro-model})
is the light client. The best chain is that of the \emph{Consensus Guide},
\S``Work, and the best chain'' (ZIP 307, ``High-Level Design'').
\end{definition}

\begin{assumption}[Honest-but-curious server]\label{ass:service-hbc}
The server satisfies two clauses.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item \emph{Integrity.} It answers every query from a correct view of the
  current best chain, each response being the value that the interface of
  ``The query interface'' (\S\ref{sec:service-queries}) assigns to the query
  on that chain, and it transmits queries and responses faithfully.
\item \emph{Curiosity.} It records every query with its arguments, order and
  timing, every response, and the transport metadata of every connection,
  and computes arbitrarily on that record.
\end{enumerate}
\end{assumption}

Every correctness result of this volume that relies on the server cites
clause~(a); the privacy results of \S\ref{sec:sync-privacy} take clause~(b) as
the power of the adversary. No other server model is used. ZIP 307, ``Security
Model'', states the same model: the node and proxy together are honest but
curious, and are trusted to provide a correct view of the current best chain
and to transmit queries and responses faithfully. Its goal is payment-detection
privacy: the proxy does not learn which transactions are addressed to a given
wallet and, given network privacy provided separately, does not link different
connections or queries to one wallet; the goal is defined in
``Payment-detection privacy'' (\S\ref{sec:priv-model}). The ZIP does not
address private spending.

\begin{proposition}[Light-client checks]\label{prop:service-verify}
Let a wallet receive responses of the service
(\S\ref{sec:service-queries}), with compact blocks that carry no header,
which the abstract format does not guarantee
(Definition~\ref{def:compact-block}). Then every check the wallet can make
compares two responses, or a response with a value the wallet computes from
responses. The relations among response fields that the format fixes are
the following:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item height continuity and $\mathsf{prevHash}$ linkage of consecutive
  compact blocks, and of the first block of a range with the block the
  wallet has stored, the checks of ZIP 307, ``Client-server interaction'',
  phases~B and~D, and ``Block header validation'';
\item consistency of the per-block tree sizes with the commitments served
  (Lemma~\ref{lem:compact-sizes});
\item recomputation of the note commitment of each accepted candidate
  (Definition~\ref{def:compact-accept},
  Proposition~\ref{prop:compact-sound});
\item agreement of a served tree node (a frontier, a subtree root, a root)
  with its recomputation from commitments the wallet has scanned;
\item agreement of a fetched transaction with its queried identifier and
  with the compact records of that identifier (Remark ``Checks of a fetched
  transaction'');
\item equality of a height, block hash or transaction identifier that
  several responses report.
\end{enumerate}
No check relates a response to a block header or to proof of work: the
wallet verifies no tree root, nullifier, transaction identifier or block
hash against a header.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The fields of a compact block and of its elements
(Definitions~\ref{def:compact-block}, \ref{def:compact-tx}
and~\ref{def:compact-elements}) and the responses of the service are values
of the chain or tree data derived from it; without $\mathsf{hdr}$ no
response carries a header, so no field, not even $\mathsf{hash}$ or
$\mathsf{prevHash}$, which the header determines, can be checked against
one. A check is a predicate on the wallet's inputs,
which are these responses and its keys, so it compares responses with one
another or with values computed from them. Items~(i) to~(vi) relate pairs
of fields that the format makes functions of one another: the heights and
hashes of adjacent blocks; a block's commitment count and its sizes; a
record's commitment and its plaintext; a tree node and the leaves below it;
a fetched transaction and its identifier and compact records; and a height,
hash or identifier repeated across responses. Proof of work is a relation
between a header and its solution and target (\emph{Consensus Guide},
\S``Equihash and the memoryless model''), which no response carries.
\end{proof}

ZIP 307, ``Block header validation'', proposes checks against a header when
a compact block carries one; the abstract format does not guarantee a
header, and no result of this volume uses them.

\begin{corollary}[Fabrication of compact outputs]%
\label{cor:service-fabrication}
\leavevmode
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item Under clause~(a) of Assumption~\ref{ass:service-hbc}, every compact
  candidate accepted by Definition~\ref{def:compact-accept} is a note of the
  best chain: its commitment is a leaf of its pool's note commitment tree at
  the position that Definition~\ref{def:compact-position} assigns. The
  fields a compact record omits (proofs, signatures, value commitments,
  anchors, full ciphertexts) are checked by consensus for every transaction
  of the best chain, so delegating their verification to the server loses
  no check a light client could make.
\item Without clause~(a), no check authenticates the compact fields against
  the chain. A server that knows one of the wallet's addresses can construct
