$t \ge 16$ are cap nodes over complete roots, and those below level~$16$ are
functions of the leaves of subtree~$\kappa$ below $n$, appended in its
extent. For the last claim, the ranges of $s_{16}, \dots, s_{31}$
partition the subtree indices other than $\sigma$; if the range of
subtree~$i$ lies inside such a carry, which is the same node at $A$, its
range lying below $n$, the sibling over it is that carry,
of kind~(e), or a cap node computed from it, of kind~(c), without the root
of subtree~$i$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Scheduling]
Scanning the tip range and the extents of the subtrees of found notes
before historic ranges is a priority rule of wallet policy, designed but
unspecified (Definition~\ref{def:intro-classes}). The order of range
requests is observable by the server (``Leakage of range queries'',
\S\ref{sec:priv-range}).
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[Witnesses under any scan order]\label{cor:tree-order}
Under the hypotheses of Lemma~\ref{lem:scan-order}, a schedule of ranges
that covers $[B, T]$ in any order yields, for every note and every retained
anchor, the witness of a scan in increasing height from $B$, provided a
note enters a witness only once the extent of its subtree
(Definition~\ref{def:tree-extent}) is scanned up to the anchor and the
conditions of Proposition~\ref{prop:tree-ready} hold.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
By the \emph{Ironwood Guide}'s Lemma ``Authentication paths from public
data'' (\S``Authentication paths''), the path is a function of the position and
of the leaves below $n$, which do not depend on the scan order. Positions are
local to their blocks by Lemma~\ref{lem:compact-sizes}, and the detection of the
note is order-independent by Lemma~\ref{lem:scan-order}. Once the stated extents
are scanned, Theorem~\ref{thm:tree-witness} makes the path computable.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Cost of a restored wallet]
A restored wallet seeds each tree from the chain state $C(B - 1)$
(Lemma~\ref{lem:tree-seed}), holds one root per complete subtree
(Construction~\ref{def:tree-cap}), and scans for witnesses only the extents
of the subtrees of its notes and the tip range, instead of replaying the
tree from the pool's first block. By Proposition~\ref{prop:tree-ready}, the
witness of a found note is ready once those ranges are scanned. The trial
decryption of every block from $B$ onward
(Proposition~\ref{prop:scan-cost}) remains, and so does the scanning of the
extents.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Reorganisation detection and recovery}\label{sec:tree-reorg}

\begin{remark}[What a reorganisation invalidates]
A reorganisation (\emph{Ironwood Guide}, \S``Anchors'', Definition
``Reorganisation'') makes three kinds of wallet information wrong: the
transactions of the wallet mined in replaced blocks become unmined; the anchors
of replaced blocks vanish; and tree information over positions appended in
replaced blocks no longer describes the chain.
\end{remark}

\begin{construction}[Detection of a reorganisation]
Each synchronisation after the first requests blocks from its previously
synchronised height $X$ inclusive and checks that the hash of the received
block $X$ equals the stored one; a mismatch implies that block $X$ was
orphaned by a reorganisation (ZIP 307, ``Client-server interaction'',
phases~B and~D). This volume extends the check to every block and to both
sides of every range boundary (Definition~\ref{def:scan-continuity}).
Failure of the continuity predicate is a \emph{continuity error} and
triggers recovery (Construction~\ref{def:tree-recover}). A malformed field,
or a tree size that is absent, outside $[0, 2^{32}]$ or below the block's
own commitment count (Lemma~\ref{lem:compact-sizes}(b)), is an error of the
response and triggers none. The extension beyond ZIP 307's comparison is
designed but unspecified (Definition~\ref{def:intro-classes}).
\end{construction}

\begin{definition}[Rollback to a height]\label{def:tree-rollback}
\emph{Rollback} to a height $r$ at which the wallet holds a chain state
$C(r)$ consists of the following changes.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item The chain state of the wallet becomes $C(r)$, and each pool's
  retained tree information becomes that of
  Proposition~\ref{prop:tree-sufficient}(ii) for $r$.
\item Every transaction mined above $r$ is marked unmined. The spend marks
  that such transactions set, on shielded notes and on transparent
  outputs, are cleared, and the nullifier of each note so unmarked returns
  to the tracked set $W_P$ of its pool.
\item Scan results above $r$ are discarded: the unlinked nullifiers of
  $R_P$ revealed above $r$, and the scanned ranges above $r$, so that the
  scanned heights of Definition~\ref{def:scan-heights} are at most $r$. A
  note whose creating transaction is now unmined loses its position and,
  for Sapling, its nullifier, which depends on the position; both are
  derived again if a later scan detects the note.
\item The knowledge of rule~(R4) of Definition~\ref{def:scan-keys} is cut
  back as that rule states; the evidence of rule~(R2) stands.
\item Local-only data is kept: sent and received notes stay recorded, since
  the memos and transactions that the wallet created may be unrecoverable
  from the chain, while a note whose transaction is unmined is excluded from
  balances and from spendability (``Spendable notes'',
  \S\ref{sec:spend-categories}).
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{construction}[Recovery from a continuity error]\label{def:tree-recover}
Let $k \ge 2$ be an offset, a wallet parameter. On a continuity error at
height $e$, that is, when a block of height $e$ fails
Definition~\ref{def:scan-continuity} against the wallet's chain state
$C(e - 1)$, the wallet runs the following steps.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\arabic*)]
\item If $e - k < B - 1$, it obtains $C'(B - 1) := \mathsf{TreeState}(B -
  1)$, replaces its stored chain state at $B - 1$ by $C'(B - 1)$, rolls back
  to $B - 1$ (Definition~\ref{def:tree-rollback}), and goes to step~(4).
\item Otherwise let $r$ be the greatest height with $B - 1 \le r \le e - k$
  that is a retained block boundary ($r \in \mathcal{H}$) or at which the
  wallet has recorded the hash of its scanned block. The height $B - 1$
  qualifies, since $B - 1 \in \mathcal{H}$.
\item If $r \in \mathcal{H}$, the wallet rolls back to $r$. Otherwise it
  obtains $C'(r) := \mathsf{TreeState}(r)$
  (Definition~\ref{def:scan-chainstate}), trusted under clause~(a) of
  Assumption~\ref{ass:service-hbc}. If the hash of $C'(r)$ equals the
  recorded hash, the wallet takes the frontiers of $C'(r)$ as its tree
  states after $r$, discards every retained node over a
  position at or above their sizes, and rolls back to $r$. If the hashes
  differ, its block $r$ is off the server's chain, and it repeats step~(1)
  with $e := r + 1$.
\item It restarts synchronisation from the query $\mathsf{Tip}()$.
\end{enumerate}
A fetched chain state extends the depth to which the wallet recovers beyond
its rollback window, but only at a height whose hash the wallet has
recorded: without that comparison, a fetched chain state cannot show that
the wallet's blocks at most $r$ lie on the server's chain, and results from
replaced blocks below $r$ would survive. The construction is designed but
unspecified (Definition~\ref{def:intro-classes}).
\end{construction}

% compute/OUTPUT.md, heights.py:
% [sec:tree-reorg] prop:tree-fork (2000 random forks, depth 1..29): a
%   continuity error at e gives f <= e - 2; rolling back to r = e - 2 at
%   each error strictly decreases r and ends with r <= f; rolling back to
%   e - 1 repeats the error at e
\begin{proposition}[Recovery reaches the fork]\label{prop:tree-fork}
Assume clause~(a) of Assumption~\ref{ass:service-hbc}, let the server's
best chain be unchanged during recovery, and exclude collisions of the block
hash. Let the wallet's scanned heights below $e$ form the interval
$[B, e - 1]$, as in a scan in increasing height. The wallet's block at a
height $h \in [B - 1, e - 1]$ is the block it scanned at $h$, or for
$h = B - 1$ the block of its stored $C(B - 1)$. The \emph{fork point}
$h_{\mathsf{f}}$ is the greatest $h \in [B - 1, e - 1]$ at which the
wallet's block is the server's block at $h$, and $h_{\mathsf{f}} < B - 1$
if there is none. Agreement at a height implies agreement at every lower
height of $[B - 1, e - 1]$: the wallet's blocks are linked by the checks of
Definition~\ref{def:scan-continuity}, and each header contains the hash of
its parent (protocol specification, \S``Block Header Encoding and
Consensus'').
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item A continuity error at $e$ implies $h_{\mathsf{f}} \le e - 2$.
\item Repeated recovery by Construction~\ref{def:tree-recover} strictly
  decreases the rollback height $r$ until $r \le h_{\mathsf{f}}$, or until
  step~(1) replaces the stored chain state at $B - 1$; after either, no
  continuity error recurs.
\item No offset below $2$ guarantees progress: a rollback to $e - 1$ keeps
  the orphaned block, and the next scan fails again at $e$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(i)~Under clause~(a) the server's block $e$ has the requested height, so
the failing clause of Definition~\ref{def:scan-continuity} is~(ii)
or~(iii). The server's block $e$ has as parent its chain's block $e - 1$,
and its sizes are functions of that chain's prefix
(Lemma~\ref{lem:compact-sizes}(a)). If the wallet's block $e - 1$ were the
server's block $e - 1$, its hash and its sizes, which the wallet computed
from the same prefix, would satisfy both clauses. So the chains disagree at
$e - 1$, and $h_{\mathsf{f}} \le e - 2$.

(ii)~Consider one recovery, with rollback height $r \le e - k \le e - 2$.
If $r$ is a retained boundary and $r > h_{\mathsf{f}}$, the wallet's block
$r$ is off the server's chain, so the server's block $r + 1$, whose
$\mathsf{prevHash}$ is the hash of the server's block $r$, fails
continuity at $e' = r + 1$, and the next rollback height is at most
$e' - 2 < r$. If $r$ is a recorded height and the hashes differ, step~(3)
repeats with $e := r + 1$ and the next height is at most $r + 1 - k < r$.
If the hashes agree, $r \le h_{\mathsf{f}}$ by the definition of the fork
point. Heights are bounded below by $B - 1$, and a rollback height that
would lie below it is replaced by step~(1). Once $r \le h_{\mathsf{f}}$, the
wallet's block $r$ is on the server's chain, and every later block of that
chain continues its predecessor. After step~(1) the wallet's chain state at
$B - 1$ is the server's and every result above it is discarded, so the same
holds.

(iii)~After a rollback to $e - 1$ the wallet's block $e - 1$ is unchanged,
and by~(i) it is off the server's chain; the server's block $e$ fails
against it again.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Reorganisation safety]\label{thm:tree-reorg}
Under the hypotheses of Proposition~\ref{prop:tree-fork}, recovery by
Construction~\ref{def:tree-recover} ends at a height $r$ such that every
block of the wallet at most $r$ is a block of the server's chain: either a
retained boundary or a recorded height $r \le h_{\mathsf{f}}$, the latter
with the chain state $\mathsf{TreeState}(r)$, or $r = B - 1$ with the
chain state $\mathsf{TreeState}(B - 1)$. Scanning the server's chain from
$r + 1$ then yields the note state and the retained tree information of a
scan of that chain from the birthday, with local-only data kept.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Proposition~\ref{prop:tree-fork} gives the end height $r$ and its property.
After the rollback, the retained information is a function of the blocks at
most $r$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:tree-sufficient}(ii),
Definition~\ref{def:tree-rollback}), and those blocks are shared by both
chains; where a fetched chain state is used, its frontiers are those of the
server's chain under clause~(a). Theorem~\ref{thm:scan-discovery} and
Lemma~\ref{lem:tree-seed} then apply to the server's chain from $r + 1$,
seeded with $C(r)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Correctness of synchronisation]\label{thm:tree-sync}
Assume clause~(a) of Assumption~\ref{ass:service-hbc} and exclude
collisions of the block hash. Let the server's best chain be unchanged from
some time on, the \emph{final chain}, and let $T$ be the height of a tip of
the final chain that the wallet obtains after that time. Let the wallet
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\arabic*)]
\item check continuity before applying the results of a range
  (Definition~\ref{def:scan-continuity}), on both sides of every range
  boundary;
\item retain unlinked nullifiers as Lemma~\ref{lem:scan-spends} requires;
\item take positions from consistent sizes (Lemma~\ref{lem:compact-sizes});
\item recover from every continuity error by
  Construction~\ref{def:tree-recover}, scanning a height again only after a
  rollback below it;
\item enter a note into a witness only once the extent of its subtree is
  scanned up to the anchor (Definition~\ref{def:tree-extent});
\item scan the range that contains $T$ after obtaining $T$, with continuity
  checked at its lower boundary (Definition~\ref{def:scan-continuity}), and
  discard scan results above $T$; and
\item hold, as its complete subtree roots, those returned by a
  $\mathsf{SubtreeRoots}$ query made after obtaining $T$, restricted to
  completing height at most $T$.
\end{enumerate}
If, after its last rollback, its scanned ranges cover $[B, T]$, in any
order, the wallet holds the note state and the retained tree information of
a scan of the final chain in increasing height from $B$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The theorem composes earlier results and adds no mechanism. By~(6) the
range that contains $T$ was requested after the tip query that returned
$T$, so under clause~(a) the wallet's block $T$ is that of the final chain.
By~(1), each block of $[B, T]$ was checked against the wallet's chain state of
its predecessor, and by~(4) no height below it was scanned again afterwards
without a rollback that also discarded the block; so the wallet's blocks of $[B
- 1, T]$ are linked by $\mathsf{prevHash}$, and by the hash chain of the headers
each of them is the final chain's block at its height. By~(4) a height is
scanned again only after a rollback below it, which discards the earlier result
at that height (Definition~\ref{def:tree-rollback}~(iii)), so every kept result
of $[B, T]$ is that of the wallet's current block; by~(6) no result above $T$ is
kept. The scan of $[B, T]$ is therefore one of the final chain without a
continuity error. By~(7) and clause~(a), the retained subtree roots are those
of the final chain with completing height at most $T$.
Lemma~\ref{lem:scan-order} gives the note state of the scan in
increasing height, Corollary~\ref{cor:tree-order} the witnesses, and
Theorem~\ref{thm:scan-discovery} characterises the notes found.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The rollback window]\label{rem:tree-window}
Consensus bounds no reorganisation depth, and node rollback windows are
local policy (\emph{Consensus Guide}, \S``Rollback limits, coinbase maturity, and finality''). ZIP 307, ``Local processing'', advises caching tree and
witness state for $100$ recent blocks, on the ground that no full node
rolls back the chain by more than $100$ blocks. That ground no longer
holds: ZIP 218, ``Block-count-based constants'', recommends a node rollback
limit of $600$ blocks at its $25$-second target spacing and keeps coinbase
maturity at $100$ blocks, and neither figure bounds the window of a wallet.
The depth that a wallet repairs from its retained information is the span
of its retained block boundaries, a wallet parameter; a chain state fetched
by $\mathsf{TreeState}$ at a height whose hash the wallet has recorded
extends it under clause~(a) of Assumption~\ref{ass:service-hbc}
(Construction~\ref{def:tree-recover}).
\end{remark}

\section{Leakage to the light-client server}\label{sec:sync-privacy}

This section states what the server of ``The light-client service''
(\S\ref{sec:sync-service}) learns about a light wallet. It defines
payment-detection privacy and unlinkability as indistinguishability games
against the server of Assumption~\ref{ass:service-hbc} and the view on which
the server decides; proves that the view is a deterministic function of the
chain, a tuple of schedule parameters and transport metadata, never of the
wallet's keys; derives the leakage of range queries, transaction queries,
transparent-address queries and submission as instances of that theorem;
and closes with a table of the server's observables and the class of each
mitigation.

\subsection{Payment-detection privacy}\label{sec:priv-model}

The server is the node and the proxy together
(Definition~\ref{def:service-arch}), under
Assumption~\ref{ass:service-hbc}: clause~(a) makes the response to each
query a function of the server state and the query, and clause~(b) is the
power of the adversary. No second adversary is defined. The \emph{server
state} $\Sigma_t$ at time $t$ is the server's best chain at $t$ together
with the state of its node's transaction pool; the latter enters only the
responses of $\mathsf{Submit}$ and $\mathsf{Status}$
(Definition~\ref{def:service-status}). An \emph{evolution} is a family
$\Sigma = (\Sigma_t)_{t \le t^{\star}}$ up to a horizon $t^{\star}$. A
\emph{wallet configuration} is a pair $(K, u)$: $K$ is the wallet's key
material, the spending, full viewing, incoming viewing, outgoing viewing
and nullifier deriving keys of every account; $u$ is every other input of
the wallet, namely the birthdays, the user's actions and their times, the
scheduling policy's configuration, any state retained from earlier
sessions, and the law from which the network endpoint of each connection is
drawn.

\begin{definition}[Server view]\label{def:priv-view}
The \emph{view} $V$ of a wallet is the record of clause~(b) of
Assumption~\ref{ass:service-hbc}: the transcript
\[
  \bigl((c_j, q_j, a_j, t_j, r_j)\bigr)_{j = 1, \dots, J}
\]
of the wallet's queries in the order issued, each with its connection
$c_j$, its query name $q_j$ of Definition~\ref{def:service-queries}, its
argument tuple $a_j$, its time $t_j$ and its response $r_j$; and, for each
connection $c$, the \emph{transport metadata}
$M_c = (E_c, t^{\mathsf{open}}_c, t^{\mathsf{close}}_c)$, the network
endpoint that the server sees and the open and close times. Times are
recorded at a finite resolution, and a run up to a horizon issues finitely
many queries, so every view ranges over a countable set.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Payment-detection privacy game]\label{def:priv-goal}
Let $\pi$ be a scheduling policy, a public randomised rule that emits the
wallet's queries, made precise in ``Key independence of queries''
(\S\ref{sec:priv-keyindep}). The \emph{payment-detection privacy game}
$\mathsf{PD}_\pi$ is the following indistinguishability game, with the
advantage of a distinguishing game of the \emph{Crypto Guide},
\S``Security as a game'', Definition ``Security game and advantage''; the
adversary is not restricted to polynomial time.
\begin{enumerate}
\item The adversary fixes an evolution $\Sigma$, whose chain may contain
  outputs to either key set, and two configurations $(K_0, u)$ and
  $(K_1, u)$ with the same $u$.
\item The challenger draws a uniform bit $b \in \{0, 1\}$, runs the wallet
  $(K_b, u)$ under $\pi$ against a server with evolution $\Sigma$ up to
  $t^{\star}$, and hands the adversary the view $V$ of that run
  (Definition~\ref{def:priv-view}).
\item The adversary outputs $b' \in \{0, 1\}$ and wins if and only if
  $b' = b$; its advantage is $\mathrm{Adv} = |\Pr[b' = b] - 1/2|$.
\end{enumerate}
\emph{Network privacy} holds when the endpoint $E_c$ of each connection is
drawn afresh, independently of everything else, from a law that does not
depend on which wallet opens the connection. The \emph{unlinkability game}
$\mathsf{UL}_\pi$, under network privacy, differs in two points: the
adversary fixes $\Sigma$ and two configurations $(K_0, u_0)$ and
$(K_1, u_1)$; and the challenger runs two consecutive synchronisation
sessions, both of wallet~$0$ when $b = 0$, the second resuming from the
state in which the first ends, and a session of wallet~$0$ followed by a
session of wallet~$1$ when $b = 1$. The adversary receives the views of both
sessions. Policy $\pi$ provides \emph{payment-detection privacy}
(respectively \emph{unlinkability}) if every adversary has advantage zero in
$\mathsf{PD}_\pi$ (respectively $\mathsf{UL}_\pi$), and provides it
\emph{within} $\varepsilon$ if every advantage is at most $\varepsilon$.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}[The goal of ZIP 307]
ZIP 307, ``Security Model'', states the goal informally and with a
lowercase ``should'': the proxy should not learn which transactions
received from the chain are addressed to a given light wallet, and,
assuming network privacy (``via Tor or similar''), should not be able to
link different connections or queries as deriving from the same wallet.
It takes the node and proxy combination to be honest but curious, trusted
to provide a correct view of the current best chain and to transmit
queries and responses faithfully. Game $\mathsf{PD}_\pi$ formalises the
first sentence and game $\mathsf{UL}_\pi$ the second; the trust clause is
clause~(a) of Assumption~\ref{ass:service-hbc}, an assumption distinct from
the goal. The chain is common to both worlds of each game. What the chain
itself reveals to a party without keys is the subject of the
\emph{Ironwood Guide}, \S``Privacy'', Theorem ``Privacy of an Action'',
whose Remark ``Non-claims'' excludes wallet and network metadata, the
subject of this section.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Scope and status]
ZIP 307, ``Security Model'', does not address how to spend notes
privately. Submission through the same server is nevertheless part of the
view of Definition~\ref{def:priv-view} and is analysed in ``The submission
channel'' (\S\ref{sec:priv-submit}). The status of ZIP 307 and of ZIP 314
is that of Table~\ref{tab:intro-zips}. ZIP 314, titled ``Privacy upgrades
to the Zcash light client protocol'', carries no specification text, so a
specified successor to the privacy treatment of ZIP 307 is an open problem
(Definition~\ref{def:intro-classes}).
\end{remark}

ZIP 307 makes two statements about its interaction of phases~A to~D
(``Scan order'', \S\ref{sec:scan-order}). First, since synchronisation
proceeds over a broadcast medium, it leaks no information about which
transactions interest the client (``Transaction privacy''). Second, the
start of each of phases~B and~D reveals the height to which the client
last synchronised, an information leak under the security model, assuming
network privacy (``Block privacy via bucketing''). The two statements hold
together: the first concerns the content of the responses, the second the
bounds of the queries, two components of the view that
Theorem~\ref{thm:priv-view} separates. The second is a leak in
$\mathsf{UL}_\pi$, since the resume height links a session to the session
that reached it; both are made precise in ``Key independence of queries''
(\S\ref{sec:priv-keyindep}) and ``Leakage of range queries''
(\S\ref{sec:priv-range}).

\subsection{Key independence of queries}\label{sec:priv-keyindep}

A light wallet delegates the storage and filtering of the chain to the
server and keeps its keys; its note state
(Definition~\ref{def:scan-state}) is rebuilt on the device. What reaches the
server is its query schedule, recorded as in
Definition~\ref{def:priv-view}.

\begin{lemma}[Query arguments carry no key material]\label{lem:priv-args}
Every argument of every query of Definition~\ref{def:service-queries} is
one of the following: a block identifier, a height or a block hash
($\mathsf{Block}$, $\mathsf{TreeState}$); a pair of heights $s \le e$ with
a pool set $\mathcal{P}$ ($\mathsf{Blocks}$); a shielded pool with a
subtree index ($\mathsf{SubtreeRoots}$); a transaction identifier
($\mathsf{Tx}$, $\mathsf{Status}$); a set $A$ of transparent addresses
($\mathsf{Utxos}$); the bytes of a transaction ($\mathsf{Submit}$). Query
$\mathsf{Tip}$ takes none. No argument is a spending, full viewing, incoming
viewing, outgoing viewing or nullifier deriving key, and no argument other than
that of $\mathsf{Submit}$ is a nullifier of a note that the wallet holds
unspent. The argument of $\mathsf{Submit}$ carries the nullifiers of the notes
it spends; it is a component of $L$ (``transactions submitted'') whose law
depends on $K$, so submission is not covered by $\mathsf{PD}_\pi$ (``The
submission channel'', \S\ref{sec:priv-submit}). Against adversaries restricted
to polynomial time, what the public data of its Ironwood-pool bundle reveal of
its keys is bounded by the \emph{Ironwood Guide}, \S``Privacy'', Theorem
``Privacy of an Action'', under the preconditions and assumptions named there;
that theorem claims nothing for an Orchard-pool bundle (its Remark
``Non-claims'') and does not treat Sapling components. A transparent address
is a receiver, disclosed by declaration (the Remark ``Transparent boundary''
below).
Which arguments the wallet chooses may depend on its keys; that dependence is
carried by the schedule parameters of Theorem~\ref{thm:priv-view}.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
By inspection of the argument types of
Definition~\ref{def:service-queries} (Table~\ref{tab:service-queries}).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Local detection and witness assembly]\label{lem:priv-local}
Trial decryption and acceptance (Construction~\ref{def:compact-trial},
Definition~\ref{def:compact-accept}), nullifier matching and the search of
retained unlinked nullifiers (Construction~\ref{def:scan-block}), and the
assembly of authentication paths from retained information
(Definition~\ref{def:tree-retained},
Proposition~\ref{prop:tree-sufficient}) take as inputs the wallet's keys,
responses already received and retained data, and issue no query.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
By inspection of the cited definitions. The underlying procedures are the
\emph{Ironwood Guide}'s, \S``Trial decryption and note acceptance''
(Construction ``Trial decryption'') and \S``Authentication paths'' (Lemma
``Authentication paths from public data''), both computed from data
already held.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Schedule parameters]
A \emph{scheduling policy} $\pi$ is a public randomised rule that, from the
responses received so far and a tuple $L$, emits the next query with its
argument, its connection and its time. The \emph{schedule parameters} $L$
collect every other input on which the emitted schedule depends: the
wallet birthday (Definition~\ref{def:scan-birthday}) and the resume
heights; the bounds, the pool sets and the order of range queries; the
subtree extents requested (Definition~\ref{def:tree-extent}); the
transaction identifiers fetched or queried; the transactions submitted;
the sets of transparent addresses queried; and the policy's random coins,
a finite bit string that fixes times, orders and connection choices. Every
component takes values in a countable set. Keys enter the schedule only
through $L$ (Lemmas~\ref{lem:priv-args} and~\ref{lem:priv-local}). Given
$\Sigma$, the law of $L$ is induced by the configuration $(K, u)$. The
endpoints $E = (E_c)_c$ are drawn from the law that $u$ fixes,
independently of $L$; without network privacy that law may be a single
point.
\end{definition}

For random variables $X$ and $Y$ with values in a countable set,
$\Delta(X, Y) := \tfrac12 \sum_s \bigl|\Pr[X = s] - \Pr[Y = s]\bigr|$ is
their statistical distance.

\begin{theorem}[The server's view of a light wallet]\label{thm:priv-view}
Assume Assumption~\ref{ass:service-hbc}, and let $\pi$ be a scheduling
policy. There is a deterministic function $F_\pi$ with
\[
  V = F_\pi(\Sigma, L, E).
\]
Hence:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item two wallets with equal $L$ and equal endpoints produce the same view,
  whatever their keys;
\item in $\mathsf{PD}_\pi$, with $L_b$ the schedule parameters of wallet
  $b$ and $V_b := F_\pi(\Sigma, L_b, E)$, every adversary that fixes
  $\Sigma$ has
  \[
    \mathrm{Adv} \le \tfrac12\, \Delta(V_0, V_1)
      \le \tfrac12\, \Delta(L_0, L_1),
  \]
  and the first bound is attained by an adversary that is not restricted
  in time; in particular $\pi$ provides payment-detection privacy if and
  only if, for every $\Sigma$, $K_0$, $K_1$ and $u$, the view has the same
  law for $K_0$ and $K_1$, which holds whenever the law of $L$ given
  $\Sigma$ is the same for both;
\item the bounds of~(ii) hold for $\mathsf{UL}_\pi$ with the pair of the
  two sessions' parameter tuples in place of $L$, the endpoints being
  independent of $b$ under network privacy.
\end{enumerate}
Privacy holds exactly when the view has the same law for both key sets,
and in particular whenever the law of $L$ given $\Sigma$ does not depend on
the wallet's receipts, its detected notes and spends, or the transparent
addresses it watches; each leak of ``Leakage of range queries''
(\S\ref{sec:priv-range}) to ``The submission channel''
(\S\ref{sec:priv-submit}) is an instance.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Induction on the transcript. Query $j$, with its name, argument,
connection and time, is emitted by $\pi$ from $L$ and responses
$r_1, \dots, r_{j-1}$. Response $r_j$ is, by clause~(a) of
Assumption~\ref{ass:service-hbc}, a function of $\Sigma_{t_j}$ and query
$j$. The metadata $M_c$ is $E_c$ with open and close times that the
schedule fixes. So $V$ is a function $F_\pi$ of the three inputs,
which gives~(i).

For~(ii), fix $\Sigma$. An adversary is, after its choice, a randomised
decision rule; write $D(v) \in [0, 1]$ for the probability that it outputs
$1$ on view $v$. By the \emph{Crypto Guide}'s Definition ``Security game
and advantage'' (\S``Security as a game''), $\mathrm{Adv} = \tfrac12
\bigl|\Pr[b' = 1 \mid b = 1] - \Pr[b' = 1 \mid b = 0]\bigr|$, and
\[
  \Bigl|\sum_v \bigl(\Pr[V_1 = v] - \Pr[V_0 = v]\bigr) D(v)\Bigr|
  \le \sum_{v \in S} \bigl(\Pr[V_1 = v] - \Pr[V_0 = v]\bigr)
  = \Delta(V_0, V_1),
\]
where $S$ is the set of $v$ with $\Pr[V_1 = v] > \Pr[V_0 = v]$ (or its
complement, for the opposite sign); equality holds for $D$ the indicator
of $S$. For the second bound, $E$ is independent of $L_b$ with the same
law in both worlds, so $\Delta((L_0, E), (L_1, E)) = \Delta(L_0, L_1)$;
and for a deterministic $f$, $\Delta(f(X), f(Y)) \le \Delta(X, Y)$, since
$\Pr[f(X) = s]$ is the sum of $\Pr[X = x]$ over the preimage of $s$ and the
triangle inequality applies term by term. An adversary that chooses
$\Sigma$ at random has an advantage at most the maximum over its choices.
The characterisation follows, since the advantage of the optimal
adversary is $\tfrac12 \Delta(V_0, V_1)$. For~(iii), apply the same
argument to the pair of sessions; under network privacy $E$ is independent
of $b$ and of the parameter tuples.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Compact responses reveal no receipt]\label{cor:priv-compact}
The response to $\mathsf{Blocks}(s, e, \mathcal{P})$, $\mathsf{Block}(b)$,
$\mathsf{TreeState}(b)$ and $\mathsf{SubtreeRoots}(\mathsf{pool}, i)$ is
the same for every wallet that issues the same query at the same time. Let
a policy's shielded synchronisation consist of $\mathsf{Tip}$,
$\mathsf{SubtreeRoots}(\mathsf{pool}, 0)$ for each shielded pool,
$\mathsf{TreeState}(B - 1)$, $\mathsf{Blocks}$ queries that cover $[B, T]$
in increasing height with bounds fixed by $B$, the resume heights, the
returned tips and the policy's coins and one pool set $\mathcal{P}$ in every
$\mathsf{Blocks}$ query, fixed independently of receipts, and the
$\mathsf{TreeState}$ queries of Construction~\ref{def:tree-recover}, with
the retained boundaries $\mathcal{H}$ and the heights at which block hashes
are recorded fixed by $B$, the resume heights, the responses and the
policy's coins; and let its query times be fixed by
its coins and the responses, not by the duration of local computation. Its
$L$ is then a function of $B$, the resume heights, the pool set and the
coins, none a function of receipts, so its compact-block phase leaks no
receipt. The commitment recomputation that replaces the discarded AEAD tag
as the integrity check of compact decryption
(Definition~\ref{def:compact-accept}, Proposition~\ref{prop:compact-sound})
is local by Lemma~\ref{lem:priv-local}. Requesting every complete subtree
root from index~$0$ is independent of the positions of the wallet's notes;
requesting a particular extent is not
(Proposition~\ref{prop:priv-shard}). A policy that drops the Orchard pool
from $\mathcal{P}$ once rule~(R5) of Definition~\ref{def:scan-keys} stops
its scanning reveals the height at which the Orchard-pool balance became
known to be zero; keeping the pool in $\mathcal{P}$ and omitting only the
trial decryption avoids this.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The first sentence is clause~(a) of Assumption~\ref{ass:service-hbc}. The
rollback heights of Construction~\ref{def:tree-recover} are functions of
$\mathcal{H}$, the stored block hashes and the responses, hence, by the
hypothesis on $\mathcal{H}$ and on the recorded heights, of $\Sigma$, $B$,
the resume heights and the coins. The remainder is
Theorem~\ref{thm:priv-view}(i) and~(ii) with the listed $L$, whose law given
$\Sigma$ does not depend on $K$.
\end{proof}

The hypothesis on times is necessary: a policy that issues its next query
when the processing of a range ends, when that processing takes longer for
a range with detected notes, puts a function of receipts into the times
of $L$.

\begin{remark}[Transparent boundary]
Query $\mathsf{Utxos}(A)$ puts the addresses of $A$ into $L$ and into the
view in the clear, by declaration,
not by inference, and a query with $|A| \ge 2$ presents the members of $A$
as one requester's. The addresses are derived from $K$, so in
$\mathsf{PD}_\pi$ a policy that queries its watched transparent addresses
has advantage $\tfrac12$ against the adversary that reads $A$. The guarantee
of Corollary~\ref{cor:priv-compact} is a property of the shielded layer
only.
\end{remark}

% compute/OUTPUT.md, compact.py:
% [sec:scan-cost] target day at 25 s spacing: 86400 / 25 = 3456 blocks
\begin{proposition}[Transparent-address queries]\label{prop:priv-taddr}
Let a wallet watch $n$ transparent addresses and query them one address per
query, in rounds that visit every address once in a uniformly random
order, at times whose successive gaps are independent draws of law
$\mathrm{Exp}(1/T)$ with $T = 86400/n$ seconds, of mean $T$, one expected
query per address per day (\emph{Math Guide}, \S``Beyond finite spaces:
countable additivity, limits, and densities'', Example ``A jittered
schedule''). Then no query argument contains two addresses, and the server
cannot link two addresses by co-occurrence in one query. It can still link
them through the connection and its endpoint when their queries share
either, and through the times in $L$: per-address randomised timing does
not unlink addresses queried over one connection. The schedule removes one
linking channel, not linkability. The schedule is designed but unspecified
(Definition~\ref{def:intro-classes}); ZIP 318, ``Transfer scheduling'',
specifies an analogous schedule, a uniformly random shuffle and
independent exponential delays, for its migration transactions.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The arguments are singletons by construction. The remaining channels are
the connection, endpoint and time components of the view, which
Theorem~\ref{thm:priv-view} retains unchanged. The law of the gaps is
cited, not derived.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Leakage of range queries}\label{sec:priv-range}

\begin{proposition}[Birthday bound from range queries]%
\label{prop:priv-birthday}
Let $m$ be the least height of any block that the wallet requests, where
$\mathsf{Blocks}(s, e, \mathcal{P})$ requests the heights of $[s, e]$,
$\mathsf{Block}(h)$ the height $h$, and $\mathsf{TreeState}(h)$ the height
$h + 1$ whose chain state below it is fetched. A policy that requests no
block below the wallet birthday $B$ (Definition~\ref{def:scan-birthday},
the minimum of its accounts' birthdays) reveals $B \le m$, and $B = m$ when
it requests a range that starts at $B$, which need not be its first range
under a prioritised order. Since $B$ lower-bounds the first block in which
any account could have received funds (ZIP 326, ``Terminology''), the
server learns the upper bound $m$ on the height below which the wallet
expects no receipt; once the wallet requests a range that starts at $B$, so
that $m = B$, it learns that bound exactly. Adding an
account with an earlier birthday lowers $m$ later. A policy that starts at
Sapling activation, as ZIP 307 prescribes for a seed that the client did
not generate (``Importing a pre-existing seed''), and as this volume does
for a seed restored without a recorded birthday
(Definition~\ref{def:scan-birthday}), reveals only that constant. The birthday
is a component of $u$, common to both worlds of $\mathsf{PD}_\pi$; its
disclosure is a disclosure of $u$ and, where birthdays differ, a linking
component in $\mathsf{UL}_\pi$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The birthday $B$ is a component of $L$ and $m$ a function of the view; the
statement is an instance of Theorem~\ref{thm:priv-view}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Range bounds and scan state]\label{prop:priv-bounds}
In the interaction of ZIP 307, ``Client-server interaction'', the range of
phase~B starts at the height $X$ that the previous synchronisation reached,
and that of phase~D at the height $Y$ that phase~B reached. Each start
therefore reveals the previous synchronisation height, the tip at the time
of the previous session, and so links consecutive sessions: in
$\mathsf{UL}_\pi$ an adversary that chooses $u_1$ with a resume height
other than the tip returned to the first session wins with probability~$1$.
Under a prioritised policy the bounds are those of the policy's ranges
(verification ranges near the stored tip, extents of subtrees, historic
ranges); they reveal the scan state, the maximum and fully scanned heights
of Definition~\ref{def:scan-heights}, to the extent that the ranges are
functions of it, and need not equal the previous synchronisation height.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The bounds are components of $L$, and the statement is an instance of
Theorem~\ref{thm:priv-view}(ii) and~(iii). For the adversary: when $b = 0$
the second session starts at the tip returned to the first, when $b = 1$
at the resume height of $u_1$, and the two differ with probability~$1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Order of range queries]
The order of range queries is a component of $L$. A policy that orders its
ranges by a wallet-dependent priority (``Scan order'',
\S\ref{sec:scan-order}, the reference interaction; ``Witness readiness'',
\S\ref{sec:tree-ready}, scheduling) reveals that order, by
Theorem~\ref{thm:priv-view}. Every such priority is designed but
unspecified (Definition~\ref{def:intro-classes}).
\end{remark}

% compute/OUTPUT.md, tree.py:
% [sec:tree-shards] depth 32, split at height 16: at most 2^32 / 2^16 =
%   2^16 = 65536 subtrees, each covering 2^16 = 65536 positions
\begin{proposition}[Out-of-order subtree completion]\label{prop:priv-shard}
Let a policy, on detecting a note at position $p$ of pool $P$, always
request the block extent of subtree $s = \lfloor p / 2^{16} \rfloor$
(Definition~\ref{def:tree-extent}) as a query that the in-order schedule
would not issue at that time. The extent starts at
$\max(c_P(s - 1), B)$ and ends at the completing height $c_P(s)$, or at the
anchor height for the incomplete last subtree; the server knows every
completing height from the responses to $\mathsf{SubtreeRoots}(P, 0)$, so
it learns $s$ and
that the wallet holds a detected note among the at most $2^{16}$ leaves of
subtree~$s$. Against such a policy, an adversary in $\mathsf{PD}_\pi$ that
places an output to $K_0$ alone in a subtree outside both wallets'
in-order schedule at that time, and chooses $K_1$ with no receipt in
$\Sigma$, wins with probability~$1$. The statement is an inference over the
abstract policy of ``Witness readiness'' (\S\ref{sec:tree-ready}), not a
measurement of any deployment; no mitigation is specified, and one is an
open problem (Definition~\ref{def:intro-classes}).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The requested extent is a function of $p$, a receipt, and a component of
$L$; the map from extent bounds to $s$ is public. Wallet~$0$ issues the
extent query and wallet~$1$ does not, so the adversary that outputs
$b' = 0$ exactly when the view contains that query wins with
probability~$1$, an instance of Theorem~\ref{thm:priv-view}(ii).
\end{proof}

\begin{construction}[Bucketing]\label{def:priv-bucketing}
Fix a bucket size $N \ge 1$ and let $\lfloor X \rfloor_N := X - (X \bmod
N)$. Phase~B requests $\mathsf{Blocks}(\lfloor X \rfloor_N, Y,
\mathcal{P})$ in place of $\mathsf{Blocks}(X, Y, \mathcal{P})$, and
phase~D requests $\mathsf{Blocks}(\lfloor Y \rfloor_N, Z, \mathcal{P})$ in
place of $\mathsf{Blocks}(Y, Z, \mathcal{P})$. The blocks from $\lfloor X
\rfloor_N$ to $X$ are checked, height by height, to carry the block hash of
the wallet's chain view, and are then discarded without further
processing. On a mismatch at height
$Q > \lfloor X \rfloor_N$ the wallet rolls back
(Definition~\ref{def:tree-rollback}) to $Q - 1$ if it holds $C(Q - 1)$.
Otherwise it obtains $C'(Q - 1) := \mathsf{TreeState}(Q - 1)$, compares
hashes as in step~(3) of Construction~\ref{def:tree-recover}, takes its
frontiers and rolls back to $Q - 1$. Both chains then agree at $Q - 1$,
and it sets $X := Q - 1$. A mismatch at $Q = \lfloor X \rfloor_N$ goes to
Construction~\ref{def:tree-recover} with $e := Q$. In phase~D, $Y$ replaces
$X$ throughout this check. Most reorganisations are
thereby handled within the same range. The construction is specified, as
the modification of phases~B and~D in ZIP 307, ``Block privacy via
bucketing'', which the ZIP describes as a proposed enhancement.
\end{construction}

\begin{proposition}[Leakage under bucketing]\label{prop:priv-bucketing}
Under Construction~\ref{def:priv-bucketing} the start of a range of
phase~B or~D reveals $\lfloor X \rfloor_N$ and no more of the resume
height $X$: two wallets whose resume heights share a bucket and whose
parameter tuples agree elsewhere issue ranges with equal starts. The ends
of the ranges, their order and their timing are unchanged, and so is their
leakage. The gain is in the set of resume heights consistent with a start,
from one height to one bucket of $N$ heights; it is not an advantage bound
of $\mathsf{UL}_\pi$, in which an adversary that chooses $u_1$ with a
resume height in another bucket still wins with probability~$1$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The start is $\lfloor X \rfloor_N$, a function of $X$ that is constant on
buckets; the other components of $L$ are untouched
(Theorem~\ref{thm:priv-view}). The last clause is the argument of the
proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:priv-bounds} with starts in distinct
buckets.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Leakage of transaction queries}\label{sec:priv-txq}

\begin{proposition}[Uncovered transaction queries]\label{prop:priv-txq}
A query $\mathsf{Tx}(\mathsf{txid})$ or $\mathsf{Status}(\mathsf{txid})$
issued without cover puts that transaction into $L$ and reveals it as one
that the wallet is fetching or tracking: a received transaction whose memo
or outgoing plaintext the wallet recovers (the retrieval of
Construction~\ref{def:scan-block}), a sent one, or a transparent
dependency. This is the set that payment-detection privacy protects.
Against a policy that fetches exactly its wallet-relevant transactions,
an adversary in $\mathsf{PD}_\pi$ that places an output to $K_0$ alone, at
a height that the common schedule scans before the horizon, and chooses
$K_1$ with no receipt in $\Sigma$, wins with probability~$1$. A transaction
identifier queried in two sessions links them in $\mathsf{UL}_\pi$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The fetched set is a function of receipts and a component of $L$. Wallet~$0$
fetches the transaction of the output and wallet~$1$ does not; the
statement is an instance of Theorem~\ref{thm:priv-view}(ii) and~(iii).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Rules of ZIP 307 for full transactions]
ZIP 307, ``Transaction privacy'', states the following interoperability
rules. A light client needs full transactions for the memo fields of
received notes and for outgoing ciphertexts, to recover spend information
when a seed is imported. It SHOULD obscure the exact transactions of
interest by also downloading numerous uninteresting transactions; it SHOULD
download all transactions of any block from which it fetches a single full
transaction; and it MUST convey to the user that fetching full
transactions reduces privacy. The MUST is unconditional, not a fallback for
omitting the SHOULDs. Under the second SHOULD the fetched set reveals the
blocks that hold a transaction of interest, not the transactions. The
rules are specified (Definition~\ref{def:intro-classes}).
\end{remark}

No query of Definition~\ref{def:service-queries} returns the full
transactions of a block, since $\mathsf{Block}$ returns the compact block;
the second SHOULD therefore costs one $\mathsf{Tx}$ query per transaction
of the block.

\begin{remark}[Timing of transaction queries]
A $\mathsf{Tx}$ query issued at a time fixed by the completion of the range
in which its transaction was detected is correlated in time with that
range; under network privacy with separate connections, the times in $L$
still link the two (Theorem~\ref{thm:priv-view}(iii)). A delay drawn
independently of that completion time would remove the correlation. No ZIP
specifies one for transaction queries, and no stated design fixes its law:
an open problem (Definition~\ref{def:intro-classes}).
\end{remark}

\subsection{The submission channel}\label{sec:priv-submit}

\begin{proposition}[Submission through the server]\label{prop:priv-submit}
Let the wallet submit a transaction by $\mathsf{Submit}$ on a connection $c$
to its synchronisation server. The server then holds the complete
transaction before it is broadcast, at a time in $L$, on a connection with
endpoint $E_c$. Without separation at the network layer it links the
transaction to that endpoint and to the other queries of the same
connection or endpoint, among them the birthday bound, the range bounds
and the transaction queries of the session. The disclosure lies outside
the goal of ZIP 307 and inside the view of Theorem~\ref{thm:priv-view}.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The transaction, its time and its connection are components of the view;
the statement is an instance of Theorem~\ref{thm:priv-view}(i).
\end{proof}

The server's operator terminates the transport, so channel encryption such
as TLS protects queries from third parties and not from the server. Every
connection exposes to the server its endpoint $E_c$ and its open and close
times, the endpoint being the wallet's network address or that of an exit
of an anonymity network or of a proxy. Without network privacy $E_c$ is a
component common to all sessions of one wallet, which is why the
unlinkability goal of ZIP 307, ``Security Model'', assumes network privacy.

\begin{proposition}[Separate circuits]\label{prop:priv-circuits}
Let submission and synchronisation use connections whose endpoints are
drawn independently, as separate anonymity-network circuits give; or let
submission go to a server that does not share its view with the
synchronisation server, in which case the $\mathsf{Submit}$ query is absent
from that server's view and the separation rests on that non-collusion
assumption. Then the endpoint no longer links the $\mathsf{Submit}$ query to
the synchronisation session. Correlation remains through the times in $L$;
through content, the anchor and expiry heights of the transaction against
the range bounds of the session; and through application-level
identifiers, such as a later $\mathsf{Status}$ query for the submitted
transaction identifier on the synchronisation connection. For payments the
separation is designed but unspecified
(Definition~\ref{def:intro-classes}). For migration transactions ZIP 318
specifies the network-privacy step (``Network-layer privacy'': the wallet
MUST offer Tor and, where available, Nym, and MAY broadcast through a
server other than the synchronisation server) and the separation in time
of synchronisation from broadcast (``Decoupling synchronization from
broadcast'').
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The endpoint components of the two connections are independent, so their
joint law is the same whether or not the connections belong to one
wallet; every other component of $L$ is unchanged, and the remaining
channels are those components (Theorem~\ref{thm:priv-view}(iii)). In the
second case the synchronisation server's view contains no
$\mathsf{Submit}$ query, and under non-collusion the other server's view
is not joined to it.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Summary of server observables}\label{sec:priv-summary}

Table~\ref{tab:priv-observables} lists the observables of the server, what
each reveals, its mitigation and the class of that mitigation
(Definition~\ref{def:intro-classes}). The interface of
Definition~\ref{def:service-queries} omits mempool queries, so no row
concerns the mempool.

\begin{table}[tp]
\centering\small
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.15}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{2.6cm} p{3.3cm} p{3.95cm} p{3.25cm}@{}}
\toprule
Observable & Reveals & Mitigation & Class \\
\midrule

  & \raggedright network location and activity windows

Every row of Table~\ref{tab:priv-observables} is a component of $L$ or of
the transport metadata (Theorem~\ref{thm:priv-view}); none is a function of
the content of compact blocks (Corollary~\ref{cor:priv-compact}). The size
of each leak depends on policies that the protocol leaves open: beyond the
rules of ZIP 307 and the migration schedule of ZIP 318, no ZIP fixes the
scheduling policy of a wallet.

\section{Anchors, confirmations, and expiry}\label{sec:spendability}

This section fixes the heights and the set of notes from which a wallet
builds a transaction. It states the confirmation policy of ZIP 315; the
target height and the anchor height, with the lemma that ties the anchor
depth to the trusted confirmation depth; the expiry rule of ZIP 203 and the
expiry height a wallet sets; the record a wallet keeps of the transactions it
creates and the notes those transactions lock; and the spendability
categories of ZIP 315, with the spendable set defined from the wallet's
state.

\subsection{The confirmation policy}\label{sec:spend-confirmations}

An output mined in a block of the best chain leaves the chain when a
reorganisation replaces that block (\emph{Ironwood Guide}, \S``Anchors'',
Definition ``Reorganisation''), so a wallet spends an output only once it has a
number of confirmations fixed by a policy. The rules of this subsection are
those of ZIP 315 and are specified (Definition~\ref{def:intro-classes}); the
status of ZIP 315 is that of Table~\ref{tab:intro-zips}.

\begin{definition}[Confirmation policy]\label{def:spend-policy}
A \emph{confirmation policy} is a triple $(c_{\mathrm{tr}},
c_{\mathrm{untr}}, z)$ of integers $1 \le c_{\mathrm{tr}} \le
c_{\mathrm{untr}}$, the \emph{trusted} and the \emph{untrusted confirmation
depth}, and a flag $z \in \{\mathsf{true}, \mathsf{false}\}$ that permits
zero-confirmation shielding. Confirmations are those of the \emph{Consensus
Guide}, \S``Work, and the best chain'', Definition ``Confirmations'': at a tip
of height $T$, an output
mined in the block of height $m \le T$ of the best chain has $T - m + 1$
confirmations, its own block being the first; an output of a transaction
that no block of the best chain contains has $0$. ZIP 315 RECOMMENDS the
depths $c_{\mathrm{tr}} = 3$ and $c_{\mathrm{untr}} = 10$ (``Trusted and
untrusted TXOs'') and permits, without recommending, $z = \mathsf{true}$
(``Transparent UTXOs'', MAY); a wallet SHOULD have a policy that it
communicates to the user in a clear form (``Trusted and untrusted TXOs'').
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Trusted and untrusted outputs]\label{def:spend-trust}
A \emph{TXO} is an output, transparent coin or shielded note, of a
transaction of the best chain or of a mempool visible to the wallet (ZIP
315, ``Terminology''). A TXO received by the wallet is \emph{trusted} if it
is an output of a transaction created by the wallet itself, which a wallet
SHOULD treat as trusted, or of an external transaction that the user has
marked trusted, a marking that a wallet MAY offer; every other received TXO
is \emph{untrusted} (ZIP 315, ``Trusted and untrusted TXOs''; ZIP 315,
``Prompt accessibility of funds''). A trusted TXO requires
$c_{\mathrm{tr}}$ confirmations and an untrusted TXO $c_{\mathrm{untr}}$.
The output of a shielding transaction is not trusted by virtue of its
internal receiver (ZIP 315, ``Transparent UTXOs''); its depth rule is
stated below.
\end{definition}

The rationale of the default depths is ZIP 315's and is cited, not derived
(ZIP 315, ``Rationale for the given numbers of confirmations''): the trusted
depth is $3$ because reorganisations are anecdotally shorter than three
blocks, and the two depths differ because the value of a trusted TXO is
recoverable after a rollback, the wallet re-creating its transaction,
whereas an untrusted TXO may need the sender to pay again. The modelled
double-spend risk of a given count is that of the \emph{Consensus Guide},
\S``Waiting for confirmations'' and \S``Wallet confirmation policy'': three
confirmations give an attacker with $10\%$ of the hash rate a success
probability of $1.712\%$, and ten about $0.0008\%$. A confirmation count bounds
the modelled double-spend probability only for a stated attacker share $q$
of the hash rate and a tolerated probability $\varepsilon$ (\emph{Consensus
Guide}, \S``Wallet confirmation policy''); it is not a guarantee.

% compute/OUTPUT.md, heights.py:
% [sec:spend-confirmations] 3 blocks x 25 s = 75 s
% [sec:spend-confirmations] 10 blocks x 25 s = 250 s
Confirmation counts are in blocks, not in time. At the $25$-second target
spacing (ZIP 218, ``Block target spacing''; \emph{Consensus Guide},
\S``Confirmation times at 25-second spacing''), $10$ blocks are $10 \times 25 = 250$~s
and $3$ blocks are $3 \times 25 = 75$~s. ZIP 218 does not argue for reducing
confirmation counts (ZIP 218, ``Motivation'') and keeps the anchor depth at
$3$ blocks (ZIP 218, ``Anchor selection depth''). Neither an equal count nor
an equal elapsed time establishes an equal risk, whose model is that of the
\emph{Consensus Guide}.

Two kinds of transaction enter the rules below; both are constructed in
``TEX transfers and shielding transactions'' (\S\ref{sec:pipeline-tex}).
A \emph{shielding transaction} spends transparent UTXOs of the wallet and pays
their value, less the fee, to an internal shielded receiver of the spending
account, with no other recipient. An \emph{ephemeral UTXO} is the
transparent output that the first transaction of a transfer to a TEX address
creates and its second transaction spends (ZIP 320, ``Design considerations
for Senders''; ZIP 315, ``Trusted and untrusted TXOs'').

The following rules are specified. \emph{Floor}: wallets SHOULD NOT permit
the spending of TXOs with fewer than $3$ confirmations, except (i)
transparent UTXOs spent in shielding transactions and (ii) ephemeral UTXOs
of a ZIP 320 transfer, whose second transaction spends an output that may be
unmined (ZIP 315, ``Trusted and untrusted TXOs''). \emph{Zero-confirmation
shielding}: wallets MAY spend received transparent UTXOs in wallet-internal
shielding transactions with zero confirmations (ZIP 315, ``Transparent
UTXOs''); the flag $z$ of Definition~\ref{def:spend-policy} records whether
the wallet does. \emph{Shielding outputs}: a shielded output of a
wallet-internal shielding transaction meets its depth requirement if and
only if every transparent UTXO that the transaction spent has at least
$c_{\mathrm{untr}} - c_{\mathrm{tr}}$ confirmations and the shielding
transaction has at least $c_{\mathrm{tr}}$ (ZIP 315, ``Shielded TXOs''). With
$h_s$ the greatest mining height of the spent UTXOs and $T$ the tip, the
first condition is $T - h_s + 1 \ge c_{\mathrm{untr}} - c_{\mathrm{tr}}$.

% compute/OUTPUT.md, heights.py:
% [sec:spend-confirmations] shielding output, default policy (3, 10): sources
%   at h_s, shielding transaction at h_s + d; 'Shielded TXOs' reading (sources
%   >= 10 - 3 = 7 confirmations, transaction >= 3) holds iff
%   T >= h_s + max(6, 2 + d); 'Transparent UTXOs' reading (sources >= 10) iff
%   T >= h_s + max(9, 2 + d) (checked d = 0..19)
% [sec:spend-confirmations] at d = 0: T >= h_s + 6 against T >= h_s + 9; the
%   readings agree once d >= 7
\begin{remark}[Two readings of the shielding rule]
\label{rem:spend-shielding-readings}
ZIP 315 states the rule for shielding outputs twice, and the two statements
differ. The paragraph ``Shielded TXOs'' requires $c_{\mathrm{untr}} -
c_{\mathrm{tr}}$ confirmations of the source UTXOs; the paragraph
``Transparent UTXOs'' requires $c_{\mathrm{untr}}$ confirmations of the
transaction that produced them. Both require $c_{\mathrm{tr}}$ confirmations
of the shielding transaction. Let the sources be mined at greatest height
$h_s$ and the shielding transaction at $h_s + d$ with $d \ge 0$. At the
policy $(3, 10, \mathsf{true})$, the first reading is met at every tip
\[
  T \ge h_s + \max(6,\, 2 + d),
\]
and the second at every tip $T \ge h_s + \max(9,\, 2 + d)$; the two agree
for $d \ge 7$. Indeed, a block at height $m$ has $T - m + 1$ confirmations
at tip $T$ (Definition~\ref{def:spend-policy}), and
\begin{align*}
  T - h_s + 1 \ge 7 &\iff T \ge h_s + 6, \\
  T - h_s + 1 \ge 10 &\iff T \ge h_s + 9, \\
  T - (h_s + d) + 1 \ge 3 &\iff T \ge h_s + 2 + d.
\end{align*}
The rule stated above is the first reading, the one inside ZIP 315's
definition of a confirmed-spendable TXO. Which reading the ZIP intends is
an open problem (Definition~\ref{def:intro-classes}).
\end{remark}

\subsection{Target and anchor heights}\label{sec:spend-heights}

\begin{definition}[Target height]\label{def:spend-target}
Let $T$ be the height of the tip of the wallet's view of the chain, the
height returned by $\mathsf{Tip}()$ (Definition~\ref{def:service-queries}).
The \emph{target height} of a transaction built at that view is $H := T +
1$, the height of the first block that could include it. Every
height-dependent rule of construction is fixed by $H$: the consensus
branch, the fee rule and the expiry height are those of a block of height
$H$, and confirmation counts are taken at the tip $T = H - 1$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Anchor height]\label{def:spend-anchor-height}
Let $(c_{\mathrm{tr}}, c_{\mathrm{untr}}, z)$ be a confirmation policy
(Definition~\ref{def:spend-policy}) and $H \ge c_{\mathrm{tr}}$ a target
height. The \emph{anchor height} of a transaction with target height $H$ is
\[
  A := H - c_{\mathrm{tr}},
\]
a function of $H$ and the policy alone. One anchor height serves every
anchor that the transaction carries, including that of an Orchard-protocol
bundle whose Actions spend no note. For a pool $P \in
\{\mathrm{Sapling}, \mathrm{Orchard}, \mathrm{Ironwood}\}$, the anchor of
the transaction's bundle of $P$ is the root of $P$'s note commitment tree in
the final treestate of the block at height $A$ (\emph{Ironwood Guide},
\S``Anchors'', Definition ``Treestate and anchor''), and the authentication path
of every note that the transaction spends from $P$ is computed to that root
(\emph{Ironwood Guide}, \S``Authentication paths''). Since $c_{\mathrm{tr}} \ge
1$, the block at height $A$ precedes the block at height $H$, as the consensus
anchor rules require (\emph{Ironwood Guide}, \S``Anchors''; protocol
specification, \S``Action Transfers and their Descriptions'' and \S``Spend
Transfers, Output Transfers, and their Descriptions''). A transaction therefore
carries, for each pool it touches, its own anchor and authentication paths, the
Orchard pool and the Ironwood pool of the Orchard protocol separately.
\end{definition}

% compute/OUTPUT.md, heights.py:
% [sec:spend-heights] lem:spend-anchor at c = 3: tip T = 1000, H = 1001,
%   A = H - 3 = 998; a note mined at 998 has 3 confirmations, one mined at 999
%   has 2 (checked for all T < 60, m <= T)
% [sec:spend-heights] lem:spend-anchor at c = 10: tip T = 1000, H = 1001,
%   A = H - 10 = 991; a note mined at 991 has 10 confirmations, one mined at
%   992 has 9 (checked for all T < 60, m <= T)
\begin{lemma}[Anchor height and confirmations]\label{lem:spend-anchor}
Let $T$ be the tip, $H = T + 1$ and $A = H - c_{\mathrm{tr}}$. For a note
mined at height $m \le T$ of the best chain, the following are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item the note's commitment is a leaf of the tree rooted by the anchor of
  its pool at $A$;
\item $m \le A$;
\item the note has at least $c_{\mathrm{tr}}$ confirmations at $T$.
\end{enumerate}
The anchor depth thus equals the trusted confirmation depth: a note with
exactly $c_{\mathrm{tr}}$ confirmations lies in the block at height $A$. At
$c_{\mathrm{tr}} = 3$ the anchor height is $A = T - 2$, and a note with at
least $c_{\mathrm{untr}} = 10$ confirmations is mined at a height at most
$T - 9 < A$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The final treestate of the block at height $A$ holds exactly the
commitments appended in the blocks at heights $0, \dots, A$ (\emph{Ironwood
Guide}, \S``Anchors'', Definition ``Treestate and anchor'' and Remark ``Old
anchors''), so
(i) and (ii) are equivalent. By Definition~\ref{def:spend-policy} the note
has $T - m + 1$ confirmations, and $T - m + 1 \ge c_{\mathrm{tr}}$ if and
only if $m \le T + 1 - c_{\mathrm{tr}} = A$, so (ii) and (iii) are
equivalent. The instances follow with $c_{\mathrm{tr}} = 3$, $A = T + 1 - 3$,
and $T - m + 1 \ge 10$ if and only if $m \le T - 9$.
\end{proof}

The rule for the anchor is specified: a wallet SHOULD choose an anchor a
number of blocks back from the head of the chain equal to the trusted
confirmation depth, the ZIP's instance being the final treestate of the
block at height $H_{315} - 3$ when the current block is at height $H_{315}$
(ZIP 315, ``Anchor selection''). ZIP 218 recommends that the depth stay $3$
blocks at the $25$-second spacing, $3 \times 25 = 75$~s (ZIP 218, ``Anchor
selection depth''). The rule is a recommendation to the builder, not a consensus
rule: the anchor rules admit the final treestate of any earlier block.

\begin{remark}[The anchor convention]\label{rem:spend-anchor-convention}
ZIP 315 counts the anchor depth back from the head of the chain, and ZIP
218, ``Anchor selection depth'', reads it as the tip, so that a $4$-block
reorganisation is needed to remove the anchor; the \emph{Ironwood Guide},
\S``Anchors'', Remark ``Recommended anchor depth'', follows that reading, which
puts the anchor at $T - 3$ with $c_{\mathrm{tr}} + 1$ confirmations. This
volume's convention counts from the block being built, $H = T + 1$, one block
shallower, which gives Lemma~\ref{lem:spend-anchor}: the anchor block has
exactly $c_{\mathrm{tr}}$ confirmations. Both satisfy the consensus anchor
rules.
\end{remark}

The anchor depth is $c_{\mathrm{tr}}$ whatever the trust of the notes
spent. The eligibility of each note is decided by its own depth
(Definition~\ref{def:spend-trust}), so an untrusted note spent at depth
$c_{\mathrm{untr}}$ is proved against the anchor at depth $c_{\mathrm{tr}}$,
whose tree contains it by Lemma~\ref{lem:spend-anchor}. ZIP 315 gives three
reasons for the rule, specified and cited (ZIP 315, ``Rationale for anchor
selection''): a rollback past the anchor block invalidates the transaction,
whose revealed nullifiers then link it to any later transaction spending the
same notes; too deep an anchor keeps recently received notes from being
spent; and a fixed depth, rather than one depending on whether the notes
spent are trusted, does not reveal their trust. The anchor is published
data (\emph{Ironwood Guide}, \S``Anchors'', Remark ``Anchor choice as public
data'').

Every Orchard-protocol bundle with at least one Action carries an anchor,
which must refer to the final treestate of an earlier block in its pool,
whether or not any Action spends a note of nonzero value (protocol
specification, \S``Action Transfers and their Descriptions''; consensus
rule). Giving such a bundle the anchor at $A$ makes it indistinguishable by
its anchor from a bundle that spends (\emph{Ironwood Guide}, \S``Anchors'',
Remark ``Anchor choice as public data'').

\subsection{Expiry (ZIP 203)}\label{sec:tx-expiry}

\begin{definition}[Transaction expiry]\label{def:tx-expiry}
Every transaction of version at least $3$ carries a field
$\mathsf{nExpiryHeight} \in \{0, \dots, 2^{32} - 1\}$, a block height and
never a timestamp; for a non-coinbase transaction, $\mathsf{nExpiryHeight}
\le 499{,}999{,}999$ (consensus rule). A non-coinbase transaction with $N :=
\mathsf{nExpiryHeight} \ne 0$ is \emph{expired} at height $h$ if and only
if $h > N$, and it MUST NOT be mined at a height greater than $N$
(consensus rule), so the block at height $N$ is the last that may include
it; the value $N = 0$ disables expiry (protocol specification,
\S``Transaction Encoding and Consensus''; ZIP 203, ``Specification'').
Coinbase transactions, which a wallet does not create, are outside the
definition.
\end{definition}

% compute/OUTPUT.md, heights.py:
% [sec:tx-expiry] nExpiryHeight N of a non-coinbase transaction < 500,000,000;
%   expired at h iff N != 0 and h > N; includable after tip T iff N = 0 or
%   T < N
\begin{lemma}[Includability at the next block]\label{lem:tx-includable}
Let $t$ be a non-coinbase transaction with expiry height $N$, and $T$ the
tip. The expiry rule permits $t$ in the block at the target height $H = T +
1$ if and only if $N = 0$ or $T < N$; when $N \ne 0$, it permits $t$ in no
block of height greater than $N$. Inclusion requires the other consensus
rules as well; the lemma concerns expiry only.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
By Definition~\ref{def:tx-expiry}, the rule permits $t$ at height $H$ if
and only if $N = 0$ or $H \le N$, and $H = T + 1 \le N$ if and only if
$T < N$. The second claim is the rule itself.
\end{proof}

\begin{construction}[Expiry height of a wallet transaction]
A wallet sets $\mathsf{nExpiryHeight} := H + \delta$, where $H$ is the
target height (Definition~\ref{def:spend-target}) and $\delta \ge 1$ is an
\emph{expiry delta} chosen by the wallet. Reading ZIP 203's ``current
height'' as the target height is designed but unspecified
(Definition~\ref{def:intro-classes}). Every later rule that depends on the
delta, the locking of notes (\S\ref{sec:spend-locks}) and the polling and
re-sending of transactions (\S\ref{sec:lifecycle}), uses the $\delta$
actually chosen.
\end{construction}

% compute/OUTPUT.md, heights.py:
% [sec:spend-confirmations] 40 blocks x 25 s = 1000 s (17 min)
% [sec:spend-confirmations] 120 blocks x 25 s = 3000 s (50 min)
% [sec:tx-expiry] default delta readings at NU7: 40 blocks = 1000 s (about
%   17 min); 120 blocks = 3000 s = 40 x 75 s (50 min, ZIP 218's scaled
%   default)
% .wip/repin/scripts/wallet_zip218.py (zips 660b3f80):
% [sec:tx-expiry] 40 x 25 = 1000 s; 3 x 40 = 120 blocks; 120 x 25 = 3000 s
%   = 40 x 75 s
ZIP 315 specifies (SHOULD) that a wallet create transactions using the
default expiration height of ZIP 203, which it gives as $40$ blocks from
the current height (ZIP 315, ``Expiration height''). ZIP 218 changes the
default of node implementations that create transactions to
$\mathsf{NU7PoWTargetSpacingRatio} \times 40 = 120$ blocks (SHOULD), to keep
an expiry of about $50$ minutes, and keeps an explicitly configured delta
(ZIP 218, ``Default expiry delta''). ZIP 203, ``Changes for NU7'', records
that change for implementations creating transactions, from $40$ to $120$
blocks at NU7 activation, and leaves the expiry consensus rule unchanged.
ZIP 315 is not updated for the $25$-second spacing. At that spacing, ZIP
315's SHOULD therefore has two readings: its literal $40$ blocks, about $40
\times 25 = 1000$~s, and the default of ZIP 203 that it cites, which ZIP 203
gives as $120$ blocks from NU7 activation, about $120 \times 25 = 3000$~s.
ZIPs 203, 218 and 401 support the second; which one ZIP 315 intends is an
open problem
(Definition~\ref{def:intro-classes}). Either time is an estimate, since
block intervals vary, and not a guaranteed period of validity.

The choice of delta has a privacy consequence, specified. ZIP 315 lists the
expiry height, with the anchor position, among the data that reveal to which
height the creator was synchronised and some of its policy (ZIP 315, ``Kinds
of information leakage''), and it specifies a common default (ZIP 315,
``Expiration height''). An expiry delta other than the common one therefore
distinguishes its creator, as a fee other than the conventional fee does
(\S\ref{sec:fees}).

The expiry height is fixed by the transaction identifier:
$\mathsf{nExpiryHeight}$ is effecting data, an input of the header digest
(ZIP 244, ``T.1: header\_digest''), so by Lemma~\ref{lem:life-txid}(ii) a
party matches a transaction, and rejects it once expired, by its identifier
alone.

When a transaction expires, nodes remove it from their mempools together
with every transaction that depends on it (ZIP 203, ``Specification''). This
is node policy layered on the consensus rule, which constrains only the
contents of blocks.

\subsection{The transaction record and locked notes}\label{sec:spend-locks}

\begin{definition}[Transaction record]\label{def:spend-record}
The \emph{transaction record} of a wallet holds, for each transaction $t$
that the wallet creates:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item the identifier $\mathsf{txid}(t)$ and the encoding of $t$, once the
  wallet holds it;
\item its fee, its target height $H_t$ and its expiry height $N_t$;
\item the set of its inputs: the notes it spends in each shielded pool and
  the transparent outpoints it spends;
\item its mined height, or $\bot$ while it is unmined;
\item the earliest height $h^{\mathrm{obs}}_t$ at which it was observed:
  initially $H_t$ for a transaction the wallet builds, and the height of
  the block in which it was first seen mined for a record entered from
  compact blocks;
\item the latest tip at which the server reported it unmined.
\end{enumerate}
A record entered without the encoding of the transaction has $N_t$
unknown; such is the record of a transaction learnt from compact blocks,
whose compact transaction carries no expiry height
(Definition~\ref{def:compact-tx}), and later unmined by a rollback
(Definition~\ref{def:tree-rollback}). The transitions of the record are
those of ``Status tracking and terminal states'' (\S\ref{sec:life-status}).
The record is information that the wallet retains, not a storage layout.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Locked note]\label{def:spend-lock}
Fix an expiry delta $\delta \ge 1$, a parameter of the definition, whose
default at the $25$-second spacing is the open problem of
\S\ref{sec:tx-expiry}. At target height $H$, a note is \emph{locked} if and
only if some recorded transaction $t$ that spends it satisfies one of:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item $t$ is mined, necessarily at a height below $H$;
\item $N_t = 0$;
\item $N_t \ge H$;
\item $N_t$ is unknown and $h^{\mathrm{obs}}_t + \delta \ge H$.
\end{enumerate}
A locked note is excluded from selection.
\end{definition}

% compute/OUTPUT.md, heights.py:
% [sec:spend-locks] unmined t: locked at H iff N = 0 or N >= H iff t is
%   includable at H; fallback for unknown N: N = H_t + delta, delta a
%   parameter (40 or 120 blocks at NU7, open)
\begin{proposition}[Locks follow includability]\label{prop:spend-lock}
Let $t$ be a recorded transaction with known expiry height $N_t$ that
spends a note. At target height $H = T + 1$, the conditions of
Definition~\ref{def:spend-lock} hold for $t$ if and only if $t$ is mined or
the expiry rule permits $t$ at $H$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:tx-includable}). Hence,
for a note that no mined recorded transaction spends and whose recorded
spending transactions all have known expiry heights, being unlocked is the
clause of ZIP 315 that the TXO is not committed to be spent in another
unexpired transaction created by the wallet (ZIP 315, ``Categories of TXOs
according to spendability''); a mined spend, which condition~(a) locks,
makes the note spent, and ZIP 315's clause that the TXO is unspent excludes
it; and an
unmined $t$ with $N_t > 0$ releases its inputs, as far as $t$ is concerned,
at every target height $H > N_t$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For known $N_t$, condition~(d) of Definition~\ref{def:spend-lock} is void.
By Lemma~\ref{lem:tx-includable}, the expiry rule permits $t$ at $H$ if and
only if $N_t = 0$ or $H \le N_t$, which are conditions~(b) and~(c); with
condition~(a) this gives the first claim. By
Definition~\ref{def:tx-expiry}, $t$ is unexpired at $H$ if and only if $N_t
= 0$ or $H \le N_t$, so for an unmined $t$ the conditions hold exactly when
$t$ is an unexpired transaction of the wallet that spends the note, and a
note is unlocked exactly when no such transaction exists, which is ZIP
315's clause (ZIP 203, ``Specification''). For $H > N_t > 0$ and $t$
unmined, each of~(a) to~(d) fails.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Unknown expiry]\label{rem:spend-unknown-expiry}
Condition~(d) of Definition~\ref{def:spend-lock} assumes that $t$ was
created with the delta $\delta$ at a target height at most
$h^{\mathrm{obs}}_t$, so that $N_t \le h^{\mathrm{obs}}_t + \delta$. If
$N_t = 0$, or its creator used a larger delta with $N_t >
h^{\mathrm{obs}}_t + \delta$, the note is released from $H =
h^{\mathrm{obs}}_t + \delta + 1$ while $t$ can still be mined (for $H \le
N_t$, or at every $H$ if $N_t = 0$), and a re-spend conflicts with the late
mining of $t$. If its creator used a
smaller delta, the note stays locked longer than necessary. Either
misjudgement lasts until the wallet sees $t$ mined, or obtains its encoding
and hence $N_t$ by the query $\mathsf{Tx}$
(Definition~\ref{def:service-queries}).
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}[Watched nullifiers]\label{def:spend-watched}
The \emph{watched nullifiers} are the set $W := \bigcup_P W_P$ of the
tracked sets of Definition~\ref{def:scan-state}: the nullifiers of the
wallet's detected notes not marked spent. A lock
(Definition~\ref{def:spend-lock}) removes a note from selection and never
removes its nullifier from $W$. A nullifier leaves $W$ only when
Construction~\ref{def:scan-block} marks its note spent, that is, when a
transaction revealing it is mined, whatever that transaction's expiry
height, $N = 0$ included; or when a rollback unmines the creating
transaction of a Sapling note, whose nullifier is then discarded until a
later scan derives it again (Definition~\ref{def:tree-rollback}(iii)). A
rollback that clears the spent mark returns the nullifier to $W$.
\end{definition}

The mining of any transaction that spends a note of the wallet is therefore
detected by scanning through $W$, independently of status queries
(\emph{Ironwood Guide}, \S``Nullifier sets''). A rule that removed from $W$
the nullifiers spent by transactions with $N = 0$, combined with status
polling that ends at the first report of an unmined transaction, would
leave such a transaction, when it has no output to the wallet, mined and
never observed as mined: no block would reveal a watched nullifier, and no
note of the wallet would be created by it.

\subsection{Spendable notes}\label{sec:spend-categories}

\begin{definition}[Spendability categories]\label{def:spend-categories}
Relative to a chain and a wallet state (ZIP 315, ``Categories of TXOs
according to spendability''):
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item a TXO is \emph{known-spendable} if and only if (1) it is unspent at the
  wallet's view of the tip and that view is reasonably up to date, (2) it is
  not committed to be spent in another unexpired transaction created by the
  wallet (Proposition~\ref{prop:spend-lock}), and (3) the wallet can effect
  the spend, by holding its spending key or by mediating interaction with a
  hardware device or another offline signing protocol;
\item a shielded TXO is \emph{confirmed-spendable} if and only if it is
  known-spendable, the wallet can construct its witness
  (Theorem~\ref{thm:tree-witness}), and either it is not an output of a
  wallet-internal shielding transaction and has $c_{\mathrm{tr}}$
  confirmations if trusted, $c_{\mathrm{untr}}$ if untrusted
  (Definition~\ref{def:spend-trust}), or it is such an output and meets the
  rule for shielding outputs of \S\ref{sec:spend-confirmations} (ZIP 315,
  ``Shielded TXOs'');
\item a known-spendable TXO that is not confirmed-spendable is
  \emph{unconfirmed-spendable};
\item a TXO is \emph{watch-only} if and only if the wallet holds its full
  viewing key, or for a transparent TXO its address, but not its spending
  key;
\item a transparent UTXO is \emph{confirmed-shieldable} if and only if it is
  known-spendable and the wallet can construct a correct redeem script for it
  (ZIP 315, ``Transparent UTXOs'').
\end{enumerate}
The following rule is specified: a wallet MUST NOT attempt to spend, in a
user-initiated transaction, a TXO that is not confirmed-spendable. ZIP 315
notes that the confirmed-spendable balance is $0$ until the wallet has
synchronised at least the nullifier set to the tip (ZIP 315, ``Reporting of
balances'').
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Spendable note at a target height]
\label{def:spend-spendable}
Let $T$ be the tip, $H = T + 1$, $A = H - c_{\mathrm{tr}}$
(Definition~\ref{def:spend-anchor-height}), and $f$ the fully scanned
height (Definition~\ref{def:scan-heights}). A shielded note $n$ of pool
$P$, mined at height $m$, is \emph{spendable at} $H$ if and only if:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item the note $n$ is in the note state (Definition~\ref{def:scan-state}),
  it is not marked spent, and $f \ge T$;
\item it is not locked at $H$ (Definition~\ref{def:spend-lock});
\item the wallet holds the spending key of the account of $n$, or mediates a
  signer for it;
\item it meets its depth: $T - m + 1 \ge c_{\mathrm{tr}}$ or $T - m + 1 \ge
  c_{\mathrm{untr}}$ according to its trust
  (Definition~\ref{def:spend-trust}), or, if $n$ is an output of a
  wallet-internal shielding transaction, the rule for shielding outputs of
  \S\ref{sec:spend-confirmations};
\item the authentication path of the position of $n$ to the anchor of $P$
  at $A$ is computable from retained information
  (Theorem~\ref{thm:tree-witness}, Proposition~\ref{prop:tree-ready}).
\end{enumerate}
The \emph{spendable set} at $H$ is the set of notes spendable at $H$, each
with its value, pool, position and authentication path at $A$.
\end{definition}

Condition~(d) implies $m \le A$ in each case, since $c_{\mathrm{untr}} \ge
c_{\mathrm{tr}}$ and the shielding transaction has at least
$c_{\mathrm{tr}}$ confirmations, so $n$ lies in the tree of the anchor by
Lemma~\ref{lem:spend-anchor}. The spendable set at $H$ is the set of
confirmed-spendable shielded notes of Definition~\ref{def:spend-categories},
with ZIP 315's ``reasonably up-to-date'' instantiated as $f \ge T$; the
instantiation is designed but unspecified
(Definition~\ref{def:intro-classes}). Clause~(a) is clause~(1) of
known-spendability: with $f \ge T$, every block up to the tip has been
scanned, and a note is marked spent exactly when its nullifier is revealed
on the best chain at or below $T$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:scan-discovery},
item~(ii)). Clause~(b) is clause~(2) by Proposition~\ref{prop:spend-lock},
with condition~(d) of Definition~\ref{def:spend-lock} in place of the
expiry height where that is unknown; clause~(c) is clause~(3);
clauses~(e) and~(d) are the witness and depth clauses of
